Thus, punishment does not always improve efficiency; its effect depends on the underlying norm and the payoff structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency depends critically on the MPCR and the prevailing social norm. In standard PGGs with MPCR > 1/n, punishment can increase efficiency by promoting cooperation. However, when MPCR < 1/n (as here, with MPCR=0.2 and n=4), punishment increases contributions but reduces efficiency, as contributions are socially harmful. For prediction, this means that the sign of punishment's effect on efficiency is not always positive: it can be negative in games where cooperation is inefficient. The paper also provides clear parameter values for player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, and information structure, making it highly informative for prediction tasks.

- source: Janssen, M. A., Holahan, R., Lee, A., & Ostrom, E. (2010). Lab Experiments for the Study of Social-Ecological Systems. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a dynamic, spatially explicit CPR lab experiment with five players per group, costly punishment is used by participants but does not increase group payoffs or efficiency unless combined with communication. When punishment is available without communication, net group earnings decrease due to the cost of punishment, and resource depletion is not prevented. Communication alone leads to higher group payoffs and more sustainable resource use. When both communication and punishment are available, the positive effects of communication are not enhanced, and the use of punishment can undermine cooperative agreements once communication is removed. The study highlights that in this more complex, dynamic environment, punishment without communication is not an effective tool for increasing efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that in dynamic, spatially explicit CPR games with real-time harvesting and five players, enabling costly punishment without communication is likely to reduce efficiency relative to the control (no punishment, no communication) due to the direct costs of punishment. Communication is a much more effective intervention for increasing efficiency. The findings suggest that, for similar game designs, the predicted efficiency with punishment enabled (but no communication) should be lower than the control, and that the presence or absence of communication is a critical moderator. The punishment mechanism here (cost of 1 token to remove 2 tokens from another) is not sufficient to offset the cost in terms of group payoff. These results are most applicable to dynamic, spatially explicit CPR games rather than standard static PGGs, but the payoff-based outcome is directly relevant.

- source: DeCaro, D. A., DeCaro, M. S., Janssen, M. A., Lee, A., Graci, A., & Flener, D. (2024). Learning from regulatory failure: How Ostrom's restorative justice design principle helps naïve groups create wiser enforcement systems to overcome the tragedy of the commons. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Facilitated groups (those guided to discuss enforcement using restorative justice principles) achieved higher group efficiency (net tokens harvested) than unfacilitated groups. The highest efficiency was observed in groups that first experienced regulatory abuse (punishment without communication) and then received facilitation (PFN treatment). Groups that experienced regulatory failure but did not receive facilitation often failed to develop effective enforcement systems and had lower efficiency. The presence of coordinated punishment systems (as opposed to independent or no punishment) was associated with the highest group efficiency. The study demonstrates that both the structure of punishment opportunities and the process of group learning and facilitation are critical moderators of punishment's effect on efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on group efficiency in public-goods-like games depends not only on the presence of punishment but also on the context in which it is introduced (e.g., prior experience with regulatory failure/abuse) and whether groups receive facilitation to discuss enforcement principles. In prediction tasks, the presence of facilitated discussion (restorative justice), the opportunity for communication, and the structure of the punishment system (cost, effectiveness, coordination) should be considered as moderators. The control efficiency (without punishment) is lower than the treatment efficiency (with punishment and facilitation), especially when groups experience regulatory abuse and are then guided to reflect and coordinate. The findings suggest that punishment can increase efficiency, but only under certain institutional and learning conditions.

- source: Koike, S., Nakamaru, M., Otaka, T., Shimao, H., Shimomura, K. I., & Yamato, T. (2018). Reciprocity and exclusion in informal financial institutions: An experimental study of rotating savings and credit associations. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment compared four treatments in a fixed-order ROSCA game: baseline (no sanctions), punishment rule (forfeiture), voting (exclusion), and both punishment and voting. Exclusion by voting led to significantly higher contribution rates and mean payoffs than the other treatments. The punishment rule, which prevented non-contributors from receiving payouts, did not increase contributions or payoffs relative to the baseline. The combination of voting and punishment was less effective than voting alone. The results suggest that social exclusion based on reputation is a more effective mechanism for sustaining cooperation and improving group efficiency than the explicit punishment rule tested here.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games, this paper provides strong evidence that a costless, rule-based punishment (forfeiture of payout for non-contributors) does not improve efficiency or mean payoff compared to a baseline with no punishment. In contrast, exclusion by peer voting (reputation-based exclusion) substantially increases both contributions and efficiency. The game structure is a close variant of a public goods game (ROSCA), with fixed group size, known rounds, and full information about others' actions. The findings suggest that not all punishment mechanisms are equally effective: exclusion based on group voting is effective, but a non-costly, rule-based punishment is not. This distinction is important for predicting the effect of punishment in similar environments.

- source: Ozono, H., Jin, N., Watabe, M., & Shimizu, K. (2016). Solving the second-order free rider problem in a public goods game: An experiment using a leader support system. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when leaders are given the ability to punish followers using resources provided by follower support (support-present condition), both the frequency and effectiveness of punishment increase compared to a no-support condition. This leads to a higher probability of groups achieving high cooperation (as measured by contribution rates) and higher average profits for both leaders and followers, especially under 'linkage' punishment leaders who punish both non-cooperators and non-supporters. The no-support condition, where leaders have externally provided punishment resources, results in weaker punishment and lower group cooperation. The findings suggest that the structure of punishment funding and the type of leader punishment strategy are critical moderators of the efficiency gains from punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling leaders to punish using resources provided by group members (rather than external resources) increases the efficiency of punishment in public goods games, as measured by group and individual profits and the frequency of high-cooperation groups. The effect is especially pronounced when leaders punish both first- and second-order free riders. For prediction tasks, the presence of a leader support system, the type of punishment strategy, and the source of punishment capital are key moderators. The control (no-support) condition provides a baseline for efficiency, and the support-present condition demonstrates a substantial positive shift in efficiency when punishment is enabled and properly incentivized.

- source: Jiang, L. L., Perc, M., & Szolnoki, A. (2013). If Cooperation Is Likely Punish Mildly: Insights from Economic Experiments Based on the Snowdrift Game. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: This study uses repeated group snowdrift games (a close variant of public goods games) with and without punishment to test the effects of mild versus severe punishment on cooperation and average payoff. In groups of five, with 25 rounds per session, the authors manipulate the cost-to-benefit ratio (r = 0.2 or 0.8) and the punishment fine (p = 2.0 or 4.0, with constant cost to punisher). When cooperation is likely (r = 0.2), both mild and severe punishment increase cooperation, but severe punishment does not further increase cooperation and reduces average payoff compared to mild punishment. When cooperation is unlikely (r = 0.8), only severe punishment substantially increases cooperation and average payoff; mild punishment is less effective and can even reduce average payoff compared to no punishment. Thus, the efficiency effect of punishment is context-dependent: mild punishment is optimal when cooperation is already likely, while severe punishment is only justified in highly defection-prone environments.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by the underlying difficulty of cooperation (cost-to-benefit ratio, analogous to MPCR in PGGs). If the control game (no punishment) already achieves high efficiency, adding severe punishment will not improve and may reduce efficiency; mild punishment is sufficient. If the control game has low efficiency (cooperation is rare), only severe punishment can increase efficiency. The cost and effectiveness of punishment, as well as the baseline cooperation rate, are critical moderators. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from design dimensions and control efficiency in snowdrift- or PGG-like games with peer punishment.

- source: Gross, J., & De Dreu, C. K. W. (2019). Individual solutions to shared problems create a modern tragedy of the commons. *Science Advances*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that when individuals can choose between solving a shared problem collectively (public good) or individually (private solution), many opt for the individual solution even when collective action would be more efficient. This leads to inefficient resource allocation and coordination failure, especially at intermediate cost-benefit ratios. When peer punishment is introduced, it increases the rate of collective solutions but also triggers mutual punishment between individualists and collectivists, which reduces overall group welfare. Punishment does not reduce free riding but instead is used in feuds between incompatible strategies, amplifying inefficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that in public-goods-like games where individual solutions are available, enabling peer punishment can reduce efficiency (group welfare), even if it increases collective action. The effect is driven by wasteful punishment exchanges rather than improved cooperation. For prediction, if the control (no-punishment) efficiency is known, enabling punishment is likely to decrease efficiency in similar settings, especially when individual solutions are a viable alternative to collective action. The findings are most relevant when the game allows both individual and collective solutions, and punishment is peer-based, costly, and has a strong impact.

- source: Liao, Y. L., Zhang, L., Lei, S. Y., Song, M. Z., Deng, W. K., & Hu, D. F. (2021). RETRACTED: Third-Party Punishment Mechanism and Corporate Cooperation in Environmental Investment: Experiments on Public Goods Game (Retracted Article). *Discrete Dynamics in Nature and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This paper experimentally compares a threshold public goods game with and without third-party punishment (TPP), using groups of three participants (framed as enterprises) making resource investment decisions toward a group threshold. The TPP mechanism automatically punishes group members whose investment rate is below the group mean. The presence of TPP leads to higher mean resource investment amounts, higher individual and group investment rates, and a substantially higher success rate of cooperation (from 55.57% to 76.85%). Regression analysis confirms that TPP has a significant positive effect on investment behavior, even after controlling for individual characteristics and measured preferences for interaction and equity (IEP).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a third-party punishment mechanism in a threshold public goods game increases group investment and the probability of achieving the public good. For prediction tasks, the presence of TPP (punishment enabled) should be expected to yield higher efficiency or group payoff than the control (punishment disabled), especially in small groups (n=3) with a threshold structure and automatic, impersonal punishment. The results are most directly applicable to threshold PGGs with automatic third-party punishment and no communication. The findings support a more positive effect of punishment on efficiency-related outcomes under these design conditions.

- source: Mussweiler, T., & Ockenfels, A. (2013). Similarity increases altruistic punishment in humans. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that perceived similarity (induced by a cognitive priming task) increases the willingness to engage in costly punishment of uncooperative behavior in a two-person cooperation game with a punishment phase. In contrast, group identity (in-group vs. out-group) reduces altruistic punishment toward in-group members. The effect of similarity is not explained by group identity, and the two have opposite effects on punishment. The study does not directly measure or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and the cooperation phase is single-shot, so there is no opportunity for punishment to feed back into increased cooperation or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that psychological framing (similarity focus vs. group identity) can significantly alter the frequency and intensity of costly punishment in a cooperation game with a punishment phase. However, it does not provide direct evidence on how punishment affects efficiency or group payoff, nor does it manipulate or report most of the structural game design dimensions relevant for predicting efficiency. The findings are most relevant for understanding psychological moderators of punishment behavior, rather than for predicting efficiency outcomes from game design parameters. Thus, its direct value for predicting treatment efficiency from control efficiency and game design is limited.

- source: Diekmann, A., & Przepiorka, W. (2015). Punitive preferences, monetary incentives and tacit coordination in the punishment of defectors promote cooperation in humans. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when only one group member is needed to punish a defector (step-level public good), groups with punishment cost heterogeneity (one strong, low-cost punisher) achieve higher rates of punishment and lower rates of defection than symmetric groups. Tacit coordination on the strong punisher leads to more efficient punishment (i.e., punishment is more often carried out by only one group member). The presence of a penalty for defectors (i.e., punishment that also harms the defector) is necessary to reduce defection rates. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but the behavioral results suggest that punishment cost heterogeneity can improve the effectiveness and efficiency of norm enforcement in such games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment cost heterogeneity (i.e., having a designated or focal punisher with lower costs) increases the effectiveness and efficiency of punishment in step-level public good games, as measured by behavioral outcomes (punishment and defection rates). While it does not report group efficiency or total payoff, the findings suggest that game designs enabling tacit coordination on a single, low-cost punisher are likely to yield higher efficiency when punishment is enabled, especially when a penalty for defectors is present. The results are most directly relevant for step-level or volunteer's dilemma variants of public goods games, rather than standard linear PGGs. The absence of explicit efficiency or payoff data limits direct quantitative prediction, but the qualitative pattern is clear: punishment is more effective and less wasteful when cost heterogeneity allows for coordination on a single punisher.

- source: Chierchia, G., Lesemann, F. H. P., Snower, D., & Singer, T. (2021). Cooperation across multiple game theoretical paradigms is increased by fear more than anger in selfish individuals. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, all_or_nothing
  findings: Induced fear, compared to anger, increases cooperation (as measured by a latent factor across multiple games, including the public good game) in individuals with selfish value orientations, bringing their cooperation levels up to those of prosocial individuals. No significant effect of emotion induction is found on punishment behavior. The effect is robust to controls for mood, risk attitudes, and other confounds. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes rather than payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that emotional context (fear vs. anger) can moderate cooperation in public-goods-like environments, especially among selfish individuals. However, because the outcomes are behavioral (cooperation, punishment) rather than efficiency or payoff, the findings are only indirectly relevant for predicting efficiency effects of punishment. The study does not manipulate or analyze punishment as a treatment (i.e., enabling/disabling punishment), but rather includes punishment games as part of a behavioral factor. Thus, while the paper supports the idea that context and individual differences can moderate cooperation and punishment, it does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.

- source: Seip, E. C., van Dijk, W. W., & Rotteveel, M. (2009). On Hotheads and Dirty Harries The Primacy of Anger in Altruistic Punishment. *Values, Empathy, and Fairness Across Social Barriers*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that anger, rather than the perception of unfairness, is the primary driver of altruistic punishment in social dilemma games. Experimental evidence from a one-shot, three-person public goods game with punishment shows that lower contributions from others evoke more anger and lead to harsher punishment, and that the effect of others' contributions on punishment is fully mediated by experienced anger. Priming participants with anger increases their tendency to punish defectors. The paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. However, it does suggest that the presence and intensity of punishment are strongly influenced by emotional responses (especially anger) to defection or low contribution. For prediction tasks, this implies that punishment is likely to be used when players experience anger at norm violations, but the paper does not address whether this increases or decreases group efficiency. The findings are most relevant for understanding when and why punishment is deployed, not its payoff consequences.

- source: Tsvetkova, M., Wagner, C., & Mao, A. (2018). The emergence of inequality in social groups: Network structure and institutions affect the distribution of earnings in cooperation games. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that enabling punishment in networked cooperation games (including PGG-like games) increases inequality in the distribution of final wealth, as measured by the Gini coefficient. This is because the cost of punishment is disproportionately borne by poorer players, who become even poorer as a result. However, the paper does not find a systematic relationship between the presence of punishment and group average wealth (i.e., efficiency or total payoff). Thus, while punishment increases cooperation and inequality, it does not reliably increase or decrease group efficiency. The main focus is on the distributional consequences (inequality), not on efficiency or total group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in PGG-like networked cooperation games increases inequality in final wealth, but does not provide evidence that punishment systematically increases or decreases group efficiency (total payoff relative to full cooperation). For the downstream prediction task, this means that the presence of punishment should not be assumed to improve or worsen efficiency based on this evidence alone. The main effect is on the distribution of payoffs, not their sum. If the prediction task is about efficiency, this paper suggests that punishment's effect is likely neutral or context-dependent, and that other design dimensions (e.g., network structure, fluidity) may matter more for efficiency than for inequality.

- source: Chierchia, G., Lesemann, F. H. P., Snower, D., Vogel, M., & Singer, T. (2017). Caring Cooperators and Powerful Punishers: Differential Effects of Induced Care and Power Motivation on Different Types of Economic Decision Making. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: Inducing a Care motive increases a latent cooperation factor (which includes public goods contributions), while Power induction increases a punishment factor (punishment in 2nd/3rd party punishment games, ultimatum, impunity). The two factors are largely orthogonal. Care increases cooperation relative to control, but does not increase punishment. Power increases punishment relative to Care, but not relative to control. No direct efficiency or payoff outcomes are reported, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that punishment and cooperation are distinct behavioral tendencies, and that motivational context (Care vs. Power) can differentially affect them. However, it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does suggest that punishment behavior can be increased by Power motives, but this is not linked to group payoff or efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly by clarifying that punishment and cooperation are separable and can be independently manipulated.

- source: Gross, J., & Böhm, R. (2020). Voluntary restrictions on self-reliance increase cooperation and mitigate wealth inequality. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that when individuals can solve a collective action problem either individually (self-reliance) or collectively (public good), the availability of self-reliance crowds out cooperation and increases inequality, especially when self-reliance is relatively cheap. However, when groups are given the opportunity to vote to abolish the individual solution, they overwhelmingly do so under medium and high interdependence, which leads to higher rates of public goods provision, lower resource waste, higher group earnings, and reduced inequality. The effect is particularly strong when there is a risk of miscoordination (medium interdependence) or when group members differ in their ability to be self-reliant (wealth heterogeneity). The study does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms in the standard sense, but self-reliance by richer group members can function as a coercive device, pressuring poorer members to cooperate.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that institutional design features that restrict individual opt-out/self-reliance options can substantially increase efficiency and group earnings in public-goods-like environments, especially when the efficiency gains from cooperation are significant (i.e., when self-reliance is costly relative to the public good). While the study does not manipulate punishment, it shows that endogenous institutional restrictions (analogous to mandatory participation or exclusion of exit options) can have large positive effects on efficiency, similar to or greater than those observed for punishment in standard PGGs. For prediction, the presence or absence of self-reliance/exit options, and the ability to restrict them, should be considered as key moderators of efficiency, alongside standard game design dimensions.

- source: Milinski, M., Semmann, D., Krambeck, H. J., & Marotzke, J. (2006). Stabilizing the Earth's climate is not a losing game: Supporting evidence from public goods experiments. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that cooperation in a climate public goods game can be substantially increased by making contributions public (enabling reputation effects) and by providing expert information about climate change. Social rewards (support in the indirect reciprocity game) reinforce cooperative behavior, and non-cooperators are socially 'punished' by being less likely to receive support. However, the game design does not include direct monetary punishment, and the outcome measured is not group efficiency or total earnings but rather the behavioral willingness to contribute to a public good with real-world impact. The findings suggest that reputation and information can be powerful motivators for cooperation in public goods dilemmas, even when the benefits are not directly redistributed to the contributors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It shows that making contributions public (enabling reputation/reward) and providing expert information can increase cooperation rates in a public goods context, even when direct monetary punishment is absent and the public good is not redistributed. However, because the outcome is not group efficiency or total earnings, and because the 'punishment' is social (withholding support in a separate game) rather than direct, the findings are only adjacent to the core prediction task. The results suggest that enabling reputation or reward mechanisms may have a positive effect on cooperation, but the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the ability to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes in standard PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Howe, E. L., Murphy, J. J., Gerkey, D., & West, C. T. (2016). Indirect Reciprocity, Resource Sharing, and Environmental Risk: Evidence from Field Experiments in Siberia. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The experiment found that voluntary sharing was substantial and directed toward those in need, especially when reputations could be built. Sharing was conditioned on cooperative behavior when such information was available. However, the presence of sharing, rewards, and reputation mechanisms did not increase contributions to the public good (i.e., did not increase cooperation in the production domain). The expected returns from contributing to the group activity, even with the possibility of receiving sharing, were not high enough to incentivize greater cooperation. Thus, the mechanisms studied did not improve group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper suggests that, in environments where sharing is voluntary and not highly efficient, and where rewards/punishments are not strong or direct, the introduction of sharing and reputation mechanisms may not increase group efficiency or cooperation in public goods games. The findings imply that unless the payoff structure makes rewards or punishments highly effective, simply enabling sharing or reputation is unlikely to boost efficiency. For prediction, if the control game has low efficiency and the only change is the addition of a voluntary, non-committal sharing/reward mechanism (with no direct punishment and no high-impact reward), the efficiency in the treatment is unlikely to increase. The paper provides indirect evidence that the effectiveness of punishment/reward mechanisms depends on their efficiency and observability.

- source: Harrell, A., & Traeger, M. L. (2025). Reputation-based reciprocity in human-bot and human-human networks. *Pnas Nexus*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The presence of bots in indirect reciprocity networks reduces human generosity, even when bots are programmed to be strong reciprocators. This reduction is driven by humans' reluctance to help bots, due to weaker norms about bots deserving help, not by a lack of reputational concern. Both 'reputational giving' (giving when observed by a third party) and 'rewarding reputations' (helping those with a reputation for helping) are less effective when bots are present. The paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but the reduction in giving implies lower group welfare in hybrid networks compared to human-only networks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the presence of non-human agents (bots) in indirect reciprocity games reduces generosity and, by implication, group welfare. However, it does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes directly. For prediction tasks about punishment effects on efficiency, this paper is only adjacent: it suggests that social context (e.g., presence of bots) can moderate the effectiveness of reciprocity-based mechanisms, but does not provide direct evidence about punishment or efficiency. The design and findings may be informative for understanding how social norms and recipient identity affect cooperation, but not for quantitative prediction of punishment effects.

- source: Shirado, H., Fu, F., Fowler, J. H., & Christakis, N. A. (2013). Quality versus quantity of social ties in experimental cooperative networks. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that allowing dynamic partner selection in a networked cooperation game leads to the highest levels of cooperation at intermediate rates of network rewiring. At low rewiring rates, players accumulate many ties, including to defectors, which undermines cooperation. At very high rewiring rates, defectors can re-attach to cooperators faster than cooperators can detach, also undermining cooperation. The ability to break ties with defectors (interpreted as a form of social punishment) helps maintain cooperation, but only when the rate of change is not too high or too low. The main outcome is the cooperation rate; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of dynamic partner selection (ostracism-like social punishment) on cooperation in networked public goods games. It suggests that enabling players to break ties with defectors can sustain cooperation, but only at intermediate rates of network change. However, because the study does not include explicit costly punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in standard PGGs is limited. The findings are most relevant for settings where social exclusion or partner choice is the main sanctioning mechanism, rather than costly punishment. The results suggest that the design dimension of network dynamism (rewiring rate) can have a non-monotonic effect on cooperation, which may translate to efficiency if payoff outcomes were measured.

- source: Rand, D. G., Arbesman, S., & Christakis, N. A. (2011). Dynamic social networks promote cooperation in experiments with humans. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment shows that in large groups playing a repeated networked cooperation game, cooperation decays over time in static, random, or slowly updating networks, but is stably maintained when players can frequently update their social ties. Players preferentially break links with defectors and form links with cooperators, which disciplines defectors and incentivizes cooperation. This dynamic network updating acts as a decentralized ostracism mechanism, supporting cooperation without explicit costly punishment. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are most directly about behavioral cooperation, not payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that dynamic, fluid social networks can sustain high cooperation rates in repeated social dilemma games, even in large groups. However, it does not directly address the effect of explicit punishment mechanisms (costly punishment) on efficiency or payoff. Instead, it studies a form of endogenous, non-costly ostracism (link breaking), which is adjacent to but not the same as standard punishment in public goods games. For prediction tasks about the effect of enabling costly punishment on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that non-costly, decentralized exclusion can promote cooperation, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or about the effect of costly punishment. The game design dimensions are partially informative (e.g., player count, network updating rules), but the absence of explicit payoff/efficiency outcomes and standard punishment limits direct applicability.

- source: Yoeli, E., Hoffman, M., Rand, D. G., & Nowak, M. A. (2013). Powering up with indirect reciprocity in a large-scale field experiment. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that making participation in a voluntary demand response program observable to neighbors nearly triples participation rates compared to an anonymous condition. The effect of observability is much larger than that of a $25 monetary incentive. The effect is strongest in contexts where reputational concerns are likely to matter more (e.g., apartment buildings, homeowners vs. renters, and when the program is framed as a public good). The results support the theory that indirect reciprocity and reputational incentives can drive large-scale cooperation in real-world public goods dilemmas. However, the study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that increasing observability (reputational incentives) can dramatically increase participation in a real-world public goods context, especially when social ties and public good framing are salient. However, because the outcome is participation rate (not efficiency or group payoff), the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The study does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or payoff-based efficiency, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the efficiency impact of punishment mechanisms. It does, however, suggest that non-monetary, reputational incentives can be more effective than small monetary incentives in increasing cooperation.

- source: Sadowski, J., Spierre, S. G., Selinger, E., Seager, T. P., Adams, E. A., & Berardy, A. (2015). Intergroup Cooperation in Common Pool Resource Dilemmas. *Science and Engineering Ethics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The studies found that, in a complex, multi-group common-pool resource game with real stakes and open communication, groups were able to achieve cooperative outcomes and avoid the tragedy of the commons, even in the absence of explicit punishment mechanisms. Cooperation was facilitated by the emergence of ethical leaders and effective intergroup communication. In both studies, groups coordinated to ensure equitable resource distribution, resulting in moderate but sustainable payoffs for all. The game design allowed for a variety of strategies, but did not include punishment or reward options. The findings suggest that leadership and communication can substitute for formal sanctions in promoting cooperation and efficiency, but do not provide evidence on the effect of punishment per se.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods or common-pool resource games, as it does not include or manipulate punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. It does, however, provide evidence that, in the absence of punishment, cooperation and moderate efficiency can be achieved through communication and leadership. For prediction tasks, this paper may help estimate baseline efficiency in control conditions without punishment, especially in games with communication and group identity. It does not provide evidence on how efficiency would change if punishment were enabled.

- source: Majolo, B., & Maréchal, L. (2017). Between-group competition elicits within-group cooperation in children. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that simulated between-group competition (BGC) increases within-group cooperation among children aged 3-10, as measured by the number of stickers shared with group members (experiment 1) and the number of stickers contributed to a public good (experiment 2). The effect is robust to age (especially for children 6+), and is not explained by gender, kinship, or familiarity. However, the experiments do not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and do not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The main dependent variables are behavioral measures of cooperation, not payoff or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does provide indirect evidence that between-group competition can increase cooperative behavior in children, but since punishment is not manipulated or measured, and efficiency or group payoff is not reported, its value for the downstream prediction task is limited. The design dimensions (e.g., player count, rounds, MPCR) are partially reported, but the absence of punishment and efficiency outcomes means this paper should not be weighted heavily for the prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Waite, A. J., & Shou, W. Y. (2012). Adaptation to a new environment allows cooperators to purge cheaters stochastically. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a well-mixed, engineered yeast system where cooperators pay a cost to produce a common good and cheaters do not, adaptation to a novel, nutrient-limited environment can allow cooperators to stochastically dominate and purge cheaters in some replicate cultures. This outcome is driven by the chance acquisition of highly beneficial mutations in cooperators, which can offset the initial fitness advantage of cheaters. The frequency of cooperator-dominated cultures is determined by the initial abundance of cooperators. The paper does not study or manipulate punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff in the sense relevant to public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or related mechanisms, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Its findings about evolutionary adaptation and stochastic outcomes in microbial cooperation are only weakly relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in human or animal public goods games. The main value is in illustrating that mechanisms other than punishment (e.g., adaptation to novel environments) can allow cooperation to persist, but this is not directly transferable to the prediction task.

- source: Skatova, A., Spence, A., Leygue, C., & Ferguson, E. (2017). Guilty repair sustains cooperation, angry retaliation destroys it. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: In a repeated social dilemma game without punishment, group efficiency (measured as group earnings and energy savings) declines over time due to increased defection and retaliation. Retaliation (increasing usage after being exploited) is associated with lower group payoffs, while repair (reducing usage after overusing) is associated with higher group payoffs. Groups with more prosocial members (who are more likely to repair and less likely to retaliate) achieve higher efficiency. Emotional responses (anger and guilt) mediate these behaviors: anger leads to retaliation, guilt leads to repair. The absence of punishment means that emotional self-regulation, especially guilt-driven repair, is the main mechanism for sustaining cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in repeated public-goods-like games without punishment, efficiency tends to decline over time due to retaliation and lack of repair. The presence of prosocial individuals who engage in guilty repair can partially sustain efficiency, but overall, the lack of punishment leads to lower group payoffs. For prediction tasks, this study suggests that in the absence of punishment, efficiency will be lower and decline over time, especially in groups with more selfish individuals. The findings are most relevant for control conditions (no punishment), and highlight the importance of emotional and social preference heterogeneity. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling punishment, so it cannot inform the incremental effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Lo Iacono, S., Sonmez, B., & Fairbrother, M. (2024). The effect of trusting contexts in social dilemmas with collective and individual solutions. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Groups in high-trust contexts are more likely to coordinate on the collective solution, resulting in higher efficiency and less resource waste. In low-trust contexts, participants invest more in individual solutions, leading to lower efficiency and more waste. Free-riding rates are similar across trust contexts. The presence of both individual and collective solutions introduces coordination challenges and inefficiency, especially in low-trust settings. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in the game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or variants. It is informative about how trust context affects efficiency and coordination in a threshold public goods game with individual fallback, but since no punishment or sanctioning is present, it does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to enabling punishment. The design and efficiency results may be useful as a control or baseline for similar games, but not for estimating the marginal effect of punishment.

- source: Mori, R., Hanaki, N., & Kameda, T. (2024). An outside individual option increases optimism and facilitates collaboration when groups form flexibly. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that allowing an outside individual option (voluntary participation) in a threshold public goods game increases the efficiency (average payoff) and group success rate compared to mandatory participation, particularly when group formation is flexible and loners do not affect group outcomes. The mechanisms are (1) self-selection of optimistic individuals into groups and (2) belief updating that increases cooperation among those who stay. The positive effect on efficiency is strongest when the baseline (mandatory) group success rate is not already near ceiling. The effect reverses if loners' externality is high (i.e., if loners still affect group outcomes, as in fixed group boundaries). No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in threshold public goods games with flexible group formation and no punishment or reward mechanisms, introducing an outside individual option (voluntary participation) increases efficiency and group success, except when the baseline success rate is already very high. The effect is mediated by self-selection and belief updating. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in games with and without voluntary participation, but not for games with punishment. The absence of punishment or reward means the results are not directly transferable to settings where those mechanisms are present, but the paper is highly informative for the effect of voluntary participation and group boundary flexibility.

- source: Gross, J., Veistola, S., De Dreu, C. K. W., & Van Dijk, E. (2020). Self-reliance crowds out group cooperation and increases wealth inequality. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: This paper experimentally demonstrates that when group members can solve a shared problem either through a public good (cooperation) or by investing in a private solution (self-reliance), the availability and affordability of private solutions lead to a decline in cooperation and group efficiency. The effect is stronger when there is asymmetry in endowments, such that some group members can more easily afford self-reliance. In these cases, less dependent members often choose private solutions, leaving more dependent members at risk of losing resources, which increases wealth inequality. When private solutions are unavailable, groups are able to efficiently coordinate on the public good, maximizing group welfare. The study also finds that third-party delegation would increase group welfare and reduce inequality, but is not often chosen by those who benefit from self-reliance.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing the option for self-reliance (private solutions) in public goods games reduces group efficiency and increases inequality, especially when there is heterogeneity in members' ability to be self-reliant. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling private solutions (a form of exit or outside option) will lower the efficiency of the group compared to a standard public goods game without such options. The results are directly relevant for settings where the design dimension is the presence or absence of private alternatives, but do not address punishment or sanction mechanisms. The findings are most informative for predicting efficiency in games with and without self-reliance options, and for understanding the impact of resource asymmetry.

- source: Semmann, D., Krambeck, H. J. R., & Milinski, M. (2003). Volunteering leads to rock-paper-scissors dynamics in a public goods game. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that allowing players to opt out of the public goods game (become 'loners') leads to rock-paper-scissors dynamics among cooperators, defectors, and loners. This dynamic sustains a stable, nonzero level of cooperation and prevents the collapse to universal defection typically seen in standard public goods games. Average payoffs for cooperators and defectors converge to the loner payoff over time, as predicted by theory. The presence of the loner option increases the average frequency of cooperation compared to standard PGGs, but does not produce overwhelming cooperation. There is no punishment or reward mechanism in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it provides evidence that introducing an opt-out (volunteering/loner) option can sustain cooperation and stabilize payoffs in public goods games. For the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may be useful for understanding alternative mechanisms (volunteering) that can also affect efficiency.

- source: Nockur, L., Arndt, L., Keller, J., & Pfattheicher, S. (2020). Collective choice fosters sustainable resource management in the presence of asymmetric opportunities. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: In a lab common resource game with groups of four, participants played under three systems: individual choice, median choice, and majority voting. In both symmetric and asymmetric opportunity conditions, collective choice systems (median and majority voting) led to lower average extraction rates and higher profits than individual choice. The effect was especially pronounced for disadvantaged group members in asymmetric groups, whose profits increased substantially under collective choice. Advantaged group members' extraction was curtailed under collective choice, but their profits did not increase. Satisfaction and fairness ratings were also higher under collective choice. No punishment or sanctioning mechanisms were present in any condition.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in common resource games with or without asymmetric extraction opportunities, implementing collective choice mechanisms (median or majority voting) increases efficiency and group payoff compared to individual choice. However, the study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are most relevant for predicting the effect of collective decision rules (not punishment) on efficiency, and less relevant for the downstream task of predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The game structure is close to a public goods dilemma, but the absence of punishment means the results are not directly transferable to punishment-enabled settings.

- source: Hackel, J., Yamamoto, H., Okada, I., Goto, A., & Taudes, A. (2021). Asymmetric effects of social and economic incentives on cooperation in real effort based public goods games. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment finds that social incentives (charity donations) significantly increase the proportion of participants who fully cooperate (complete 90+ tasks), while economic incentives (personal monetary reward) significantly reduce the proportion of full defectors (complete 10 or fewer tasks). The effects are asymmetric: social incentives promote high cooperation, economic incentives reduce non-cooperation. No direct measures of efficiency or group payoff are reported. The study does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, focusing instead on positive incentives.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. The design is a close variant of a PGG, using real effort instead of resource allocation, and focuses on the effects of positive (economic and social) incentives on cooperation and defection rates. For prediction tasks about punishment and efficiency, this paper is not directly informative, but it does suggest that incentive structures (other than punishment) can have asymmetric effects on cooperation and defection. The absence of punishment and the focus on non-payoff behavioral outcomes limit its direct applicability to the prediction of efficiency under punishment.

- source: Capraro, V., Jordan, J. J., & Rand, D. G. (2014). Heuristics guide the implementation of social preferences in one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma experiments. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main findings are that in a one-shot, continuous-strategy PD, cooperation is trimodally distributed (none, half, all). Increasing the benefit-to-cost ratio (b/c) increases the average amount of cooperation by shifting people from no cooperation to full cooperation, but does not affect the proportion of 50% cooperators. The results suggest that both efficiency concerns and heuristic reasoning (such as fairness heuristics) guide cooperation. There is no analysis of punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. The study is relevant for understanding how b/c affects cooperation in two-player, one-shot, continuous public goods settings, but does not inform about punishment or efficiency effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it does provide informative evidence about how the marginal per-capita return (b/c) affects cooperation rates in a two-player, one-shot, continuous-contribution setting without punishment. It shows that higher b/c increases cooperation, but a substantial fraction of participants use heuristics (e.g., giving half) regardless of b/c. Since there is no punishment or efficiency outcome reported, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be useful for understanding baseline cooperation as a function of b/c in the absence of punishment.

- source: Jordan, J. J., Rand, D. G., Arbesman, S., Fowler, J. H., & Christakis, N. A. (2013). Contagion of Cooperation in Static and Fluid Social Networks. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in fixed and viscous networks, both cooperation and defection are contagious: individuals are more likely to switch to cooperation (or defection) if their neighbors did so in the previous round. In fluid networks, where players can more frequently change their partners, cooperation is not initially contagious—players are likely to cooperate regardless of their neighbors' behavior, likely to attract new cooperative partners. As the network stabilizes and becomes less fluid, contagion of cooperation emerges. Defection remains contagious in all network types. The paper does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes, and does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or variants, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its main contribution is to show how network structure and partner choice affect the contagion of cooperation and defection in binary cooperation games. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, except as context for how network fluidity and partner choice may interact with behavioral contagion. It does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Buchan, N. R., Grimalda, G., Wilson, R., Brewer, M., Fatas, E., & Foddy, M. (2009). Globalization and human cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that both country-level and individual-level globalization are strongly positively associated with contributions to the global public good (World account) in a multilevel public goods experiment. There is no evidence that globalization is associated with increased local cooperation. The experiment does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not analyze efficiency or group payoff as an outcome. The main finding is that globalization predicts broader, more cosmopolitan cooperation, as measured by willingness to contribute to a global public good.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it analyze efficiency, group payoff, or related payoff-based outcomes. Its findings are about the relationship between globalization and willingness to contribute to global versus local public goods, not about the effect of game design dimensions or punishment on efficiency. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about the efficiency impact of punishment in public goods games.

- source: Otten, K., Frey, U. J., Buskens, V., Przepiorka, W., & Ellemers, N. (2022). Human cooperation in changing groups in a large-scale public goods game. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that the number of newcomers in a group is negatively associated with the average percentage of resources contributed to the public good. This effect is strongest in small groups and at later time periods. The lower group contribution is primarily due to newcomers contributing less than incumbents, though their contributions increase as they spend more time in the group. The difference between newcomers and incumbents is partially explained by differences in private good levels and public good levels, but remains significant even after controlling for these factors. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment, and there is no evidence of sanctioning mechanisms being central to the game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. It does, however, provide informative context about how group composition changes (newcomer entry) can reduce cooperation (as measured by contribution rates), especially in the absence of punishment or exclusion. For prediction tasks involving efficiency, this paper is only indirectly relevant, as it does not report payoff-based outcomes or manipulate punishment. It may be useful for understanding baseline cooperation dynamics in large, dynamic groups without punishment.

- source: Kelly, D., Claessens, S., Sibley, C. G., Chaudhuri, A., & Atkinson, Q. (2022). Cooperative phenotype predicts climate change belief and pro-environmental behaviour. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that individuals who behave more cooperatively in a suite of economic games (including a public goods game) are more likely to believe in climate change and to report pro-environmental behaviors. The relationship between cooperative phenotype and climate change belief/behavior is robust to many demographic and personality controls, but is attenuated by political party support. Mediation analyses suggest that climate change belief may mediate the effect of cooperative phenotype on pro-environmental behavior. However, the study does not analyze or report on efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment or sanctions in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. While it uses a public goods game as part of a battery to estimate cooperative phenotype, it does not manipulate or report on punishment, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.

- source: Wu, J. J., Zhang, B. Y., Zhou, Z. X., He, Q. Q., Zheng, X. D., Cressman, R., & Tao, Y. (2009). Costly punishment does not always increase cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that, in a repeated two-player Prisoner's Dilemma with Chinese participants, the introduction of costly punishment either had no significant effect or reduced the frequency of cooperation, and led to a significant decrease in average group payoff (efficiency) in one treatment. In the other treatment, the decrease in payoff was not statistically significant. The results are contrasted with previous US-based studies, where costly punishment increased cooperation but did not improve group payoff. The authors attribute the difference to cultural factors, particularly attitudes toward reputation and authority. The study provides direct evidence that costly punishment does not always increase cooperation or efficiency, and may even reduce them depending on context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in repeated two-player Prisoner's Dilemma games (an adjacent but not exact variant of the public goods game), enabling costly punishment can reduce group efficiency (average payoff), especially when cooperation does not increase. The effect of punishment on efficiency is not universally positive and may depend on cultural context and game structure. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment does not guarantee higher efficiency, and in some settings (e.g., repeated dyadic games, certain cultures), it may even lower it. The paper also highlights that increased cooperation does not always translate to higher efficiency if punishment costs offset cooperative gains.

- source: Dreber, A., Rand, D. G., Fudenberg, D., & Nowak, M. A. (2008). Winners don't punish. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that introducing a costly punishment option in a repeated Prisoner's Dilemma increases the frequency of cooperation but does not increase the average payoff of the group. In both parameterizations (benefit/cost ratios of 2 and 3), there is no significant difference in average payoff between the control (no punishment) and treatment (punishment allowed) conditions. Individuals who use punishment more frequently have lower total payoffs, and the most successful players do not use punishment at all. The results suggest that, in repeated dyadic cooperation games, punishment is maladaptive from an efficiency perspective.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated dyadic cooperation games with direct reciprocity, enabling costly punishment does not improve group efficiency compared to a control without punishment. The cost of punishment cancels out the gains from increased cooperation. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, in similar repeated dyadic games, the efficiency with punishment enabled will be similar to or lower than the control efficiency, even if cooperation rates rise. The findings are most directly applicable to repeated Prisoner's Dilemma designs, but the mechanism (costs of punishment offsetting cooperation gains) is likely relevant for other repeated social dilemma games with similar structure.

- source: Ule, A., Schram, A., Riedl, A., & Cason, T. N. (2009). Indirect Punishment and Generosity Toward Strangers. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment compares two treatments of an indirect helping game: one where indirect punishment is only symbolic (no material effect on the recipient), and one where punishment is harmful (recipient loses 250 francs). In both, helping is costly and beneficial, and donors can observe recipients' recent behavior. The main finding is that, although the frequency of helping does not differ significantly between treatments, the availability of harmful punishment reduces the payoffs of defectors and increases the payoffs of rewarders and cautious defectors. In the control, defectors earn most; with harmful punishment, rewarders and cautious defectors earn more than defectors. Harmful punishment is rare but targeted at defectors, and this is sufficient to change the payoff structure in favor of cooperation-supporting strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that enabling costly, effective punishment in an indirect reciprocity environment (adjacent to PGG) increases the average payoff of cooperative strategies relative to defectors, even if overall cooperation rates do not rise. For prediction, this suggests that, in games where punishment is enabled and is materially costly to the recipient, group efficiency or average payoff can increase relative to a control with only symbolic or no punishment, primarily by reducing defectors' payoffs. The effect is not driven by increased cooperation rates but by the deterrent effect of punishment on defectors' earnings. The design is not a standard PGG but is closely related in its focus on cooperation, punishment, and payoff outcomes.

- source: Li, X. L., Jusup, M., Wang, Z., Li, H. J., Shi, L., Podobnik, B., Stanley, H. E., Havlin, S., & Boccaletti, S. (2018). Punishment diminishes the benefits of network reciprocity in social dilemma experiments. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment compared three treatments: (1) a well-mixed repeated PD (control), (2) a static network PD with two neighbors (network reciprocity), and (3) a static network PD with punishment available. Network reciprocity increased both cooperation and payoff relative to the well-mixed control. However, adding punishment did not further increase cooperation and actually reduced the median payoff per round back to the control level. Punishment also reduced assortment (clustering of cooperators) and, in larger neighborhoods, erased the positive correlation between cooperation and payoff. Thus, punishment in this setting is ineffective or even counterproductive for group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated prisoner's dilemma games on static networks (with two or four neighbors), enabling costly punishment (with a 1:4 cost-to-impact ratio) does not improve and may even reduce group efficiency compared to the same game without punishment. The negative effect is especially clear in payoff-based outcomes: median payoff per round is lower with punishment than without, and the benefit of network reciprocity is erased. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, in similar networked PD or adjacent PGG-like environments, adding punishment may not increase efficiency and could reduce it, especially as neighborhood size increases. The findings are most directly applicable to static network PDs with small groups, no communication, and fixed payoff matrices.

- source: Lergetporer, P., Angerer, S., Glätzle-Rützler, D., & Sutter, M. (2014). Third-party punishment increases cooperation in children through (misaligned) expectations and conditional cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that the threat of third-party punishment in a one-shot PD with children increases cooperation rates from 25% (control) to 58% (punishment treatment). This leads to an increase in average payoffs from 2.49 tokens (control) to 2.98 tokens (punishment), a 20% improvement in efficiency. The effect is not due to frequent actual punishment (which is rare), but rather to children's (over-)expectation of punishment and to conditional cooperation based on higher expected partner cooperation. The effect is robust across age cohorts (7-11 years).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling third-party punishment in a two-player, one-shot PD with children substantially increases efficiency, even when actual punishment is rare. The effect is mediated by beliefs and conditional cooperation. For prediction, the presence of a third-party punishment mechanism (with a strong, binary punishment technology) in a simple, anonymous, one-shot, two-player game with no communication and known payoffs can be expected to increase efficiency by a substantial margin (here, ~20%). The effect is robust to age (within 7-11), and is not dependent on actual punishment frequency. The design is adjacent to a PGG, but the payoff structure and mechanism are closely related.
