  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that human players, when interacting with individualistic (extortionate) computerized agents in an iterated UG/IPD setting, are unable to avoid being exploited, leading to significantly lower evolutionary fitness compared to their opponents. In contrast, interactions with prosocial agents result in higher mutual payoffs and more stable cooperation. Over time, cooperation erodes, and human players are at risk of 'extinction' (i.e., being outperformed in payoff terms) in 70% of cases against individualistic agents. The paper demonstrates that the structure of the opponent's strategy (prosocial vs. individualistic) is a key moderator of efficiency outcomes, but does not directly manipulate or analyze punishment mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in iterated dyadic games structurally adjacent to public goods games, the presence of extortionate (individualistic) strategies leads to lower efficiency and higher risk of poor payoff outcomes for cooperative human players. While explicit punishment is not present, the findings are relevant for predicting efficiency in environments where exploitation or extortion is possible. The results suggest that, absent effective countermeasures (such as punishment or reputation), efficiency will be lower when players face individualistic, non-cooperative strategies. For the prediction task, this paper supports the expectation that, in the absence of punishment, efficiency will be low when extortionate strategies are possible, and that mechanisms to counteract exploitation (such as punishment) may be necessary to sustain high efficiency.

- source: Raihani, N. J., & Barclay, P. (2016). Exploring the trade-off between quality and fairness in human partner choice. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: When given a choice between a rich-stingy and a poor-fair partner in a modified Dictator Game, choosers often select the fair partner, even at a cost to their own expected pay-off. This tendency is stronger when the wealth of partners is unstable. When both partners are equally fair or unfair, choosers prefer the richer partner, aligning with maximizing expected pay-off. The findings indicate that fairness is prioritized over ability (wealth) in partner choice, especially under uncertainty, and that individuals are willing to incur personal costs to avoid rewarding unfairness.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in environments where individuals can choose partners, people may sacrifice efficiency (personal or group payoff) to avoid unfair partners, especially when partner quality is unstable. While not a public goods game, the findings are relevant for predicting that the introduction of partner choice or similar sanctioning mechanisms may not always increase efficiency—if individuals use these mechanisms to punish unfairness even at a cost to themselves. The results suggest that the effect of punishment or exclusion on efficiency may be less positive or even negative when individuals are motivated by fairness rather than payoff maximization.

- source: dos Santos, M., Placì, S., & Wedekind, C. (2015). Stochasticity in economic losses increases the value of reputation in indirect reciprocity. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that stochasticity in economic losses does not increase mean helping rates or mean group earnings, but it does increase the payoff advantage of having a good reputation. Under stochastic losses, generous players are helped more and selfish players are helped less, leading to a steeper positive relationship between generosity and final earnings. In stable environments, this relationship is absent. Thus, stochasticity increases the efficiency of reputation-based cooperation by making investment in reputation more beneficial, even though overall group efficiency does not increase.
  prediction_guidance: This paper suggests that, in repeated helping games with indirect reciprocity, environmental stochasticity (randomness in losses) increases the payoff return to cooperative behavior (reputation), even if mean group efficiency does not change. For prediction tasks, this implies that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (here, reputation-based exclusion or helping) on efficiency may be more positive in environments with stochastic payoffs or risks, because the social system more effectively rewards cooperators and penalizes defectors. However, the game is not a standard public goods game and does not feature explicit costly punishment; rather, it is an indirect reciprocity helping game. The findings are most relevant for settings where reputation or exclusion is the main sanctioning mechanism, and where environmental risk is present.

- source: Wang, J., Suri, S., & Watts, D. J. (2012). Cooperation and assortativity with dynamic partner updating. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that dynamic partner updating—where players can choose to make or break ties with others—substantially increases both cooperation rates and average payoffs in iterated prisoner's dilemma games, compared to static networks. Even low rates of partner updating are sufficient to produce large positive effects. The effect is concave: initial increases in update rate have large effects, but further increases yield smaller gains. When the payoff matrix is modified so that maintaining ties with defectors is more costly, cooperation is even higher and more sustained, as players are more willing to sever ties with defectors. The main mechanism is assortative mixing: cooperators preferentially connect with other cooperators and avoid defectors, rather than punishing defectors directly. The results suggest that dynamic partner selection can substitute for explicit punishment or reward mechanisms in promoting cooperation and increasing group payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling dynamic partner updating (i.e., endogenous, bilateral partner choice and unilateral link deletion) in repeated social dilemma games can dramatically increase group efficiency (average payoff relative to the cooperative optimum), even in the absence of explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. The effect is robust across a range of update rates and is further enhanced when the cost of interacting with defectors is increased. For prediction tasks, the presence of dynamic partner updating should be treated as a strong positive moderator of efficiency, especially when players can freely choose partners and sever ties. However, the game is an iterated prisoner's dilemma on a dynamic network, not a standard public goods game, and the 'punishment' is implicit (via partner selection) rather than explicit. Thus, while the findings are highly informative for environments with endogenous partner choice, they are only adjacent to explicit punishment in PGGs. The results suggest that, in settings where partner choice is possible, explicit punishment may be less necessary for achieving high efficiency.

- source: Rustagi, D., Engel, S., & Kosfeld, M. (2010). Conditional Cooperation and Costly Monitoring Explain Success in Forest Commons Management. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that (1) groups with a higher share of conditional cooperators have significantly better forest management outcomes, (2) costly monitoring (enforcement) is more prevalent in groups with more conditional cooperators, and (3) both conditional cooperation and costly monitoring are important for successful commons management. The main outcome is a real-world efficiency proxy (forest management success), not laboratory earnings. The public goods game is used to measure behavioral types, not as the main outcome. The study does not experimentally manipulate punishment or monitoring, but observes natural variation in monitoring (enforcement) and links it to outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence that costly monitoring (enforcement) is associated with higher group-level efficiency in a real-world commons context, and that the presence of conditional cooperators amplifies this effect. While the study does not manipulate punishment in a controlled PGG, it supports the idea that enabling or increasing costly enforcement (monitoring and punishment) can improve group efficiency, especially when conditional cooperators are present. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence and intensity of enforcement mechanisms (even if costly) are likely to increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments, particularly in groups with more conditional cooperators. However, the evidence is observational and context-specific (forest management), not from a lab PGG with toggled punishment.

- source: Wang, R. W., Sun, B. F., Zheng, Q., Shi, L., & Zhu, L. X. (2011). Asymmetric interaction and indeterminate fitness correlation between cooperative partners in the fig-fig wasp mutualism. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The study finds that asymmetric interference competition among cooperative actors (wasps) can stabilize cooperation by keeping resource use unsaturated, leading to positive fitness correlations (mutual benefit) between partners. When interference competition is prevented (sequential entry), or when wasp oviposition efficiency is high (e.g., in cold season), the system can shift to negative fitness correlation (conflict), indicating overexploitation and reduced group efficiency. The relationship between cooperation and efficiency is not fixed but varies stochastically with environmental and experimental factors. The paper also discusses the role of discriminative sanctions (host reducing egg deposition efficiency of less-cooperative wasps) and rewards in maintaining cooperation, but these are not directly manipulated as in PGG punishment/reward treatments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task. It shows that mechanisms analogous to punishment (sanctions by the host) and interference competition among cooperators can maintain or undermine group efficiency, depending on ecological context and the structure of interaction. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms on efficiency is highly sensitive to the details of resource competition, the number of actors, and environmental conditions. However, the system studied is a biological mutualism, not a PGG or direct human social dilemma, and the 'punishment' is not implemented as a discrete, costly action by peers but as host-imposed sanctions. Thus, while the results are adjacent and informative, they should be transferred to PGG predictions with caution, especially regarding the direct effect of enabling punishment on efficiency.

- source: Halevy, N., & Halali, E. (2015). Selfish third parties act as peacemakers by transforming conflicts and promoting cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The main findings are: (1) The possibility and higher probability of third-party intervention increase cooperation rates among disputants in both interpersonal and intergroup contexts. (2) Third parties' willingness to intervene is highly sensitive to the risk and potential rewards of intervention; reducing risk or increasing potential gain increases intervention rates. (3) Despite the potential for increased collective gains, third parties often intervene less frequently than would be optimal, leading to missed opportunities for higher group payoffs. (4) The study does not directly report efficiency or group payoff as primary outcomes, but discusses them in the context of missed opportunities and the structure of the game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the introduction of a punishment/reward mechanism (via third-party intervention) can increase cooperation rates in social dilemma-like settings, even when the underlying game is not a standard public goods game. However, because the primary outcomes are behavioral (cooperation/intervention rates) and not direct measures of efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects. The results suggest that the design of punishment/reward mechanisms (including the risk and incentive structure for enforcers) is critical for their effectiveness. The evidence is most relevant for adjacent settings where third-party intervention is possible, but less directly transferable to standard PGGs with endogenous peer punishment.

- source: Burum, B., Nowak, M. A., & Hoffman, M. (2020). An evolutionary explanation for ineffective altruism. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that people are generally insensitive to the efficacy (impact) of their charitable giving, in contrast to their sensitivity when making decisions for themselves or family. Social rewards (reputation, praise, monetary reward from third parties) are given for the act of giving, not for giving effectively or giving more. This insensitivity is not due to cognitive or emotional limitations, but rather to the evolutionary logic that social rewards are easier to coordinate around observable, well-defined actions (like giving) than around less observable or harder-to-define features (like efficacy or amount). The studies do not use public goods games or punishment, but the reward mechanism is conceptually adjacent to sanctioning. No efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it offers indirect evidence that social incentives (rewards) are most effective when they are based on observable, well-defined actions, and that people are not motivated by efficiency or impact when giving is socially rewarded. The findings suggest that, in environments where social rewards or punishments are based on easily observed behaviors (like contributing at all), these incentives may not improve efficiency if they do not target effective or high-impact contributions. The lack of sensitivity to efficacy in social rewards may limit the effectiveness of punishment or reward mechanisms in increasing group efficiency, unless the mechanisms are designed to be sensitive to impact. The paper is most relevant for understanding the limitations of social incentives in promoting efficient cooperation, rather than for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGGs with punishment.

- source: Cuesta, J. A., Gracia-Lázaro, C., Ferrer, A., Moreno, Y., & Sánchez, A. (2015). Reputation drives cooperative behaviour and network formation in human groups. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiments show that providing information about others' past actions (reputation/memory) leads to higher and more stable cooperation rates in a dynamic network Prisoner's Dilemma. When no memory is available, cooperation decays and the network becomes highly connected (as players have no reason to avoid defectors). With memory, players break links with defectors, leading to lower connectivity but higher cooperation. The effect is stronger as the memory window increases. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so the direct effect on efficiency is not measured. The 'punishment' mechanism is the breaking of links, not a costly punishment as in standard PGG experiments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of reputation-based partner selection (link breaking) on cooperation in dynamic network games. While the setting is an iterated Prisoner's Dilemma (not a PGG), and the 'punishment' is not costly, the results suggest that allowing players to sanction defectors by breaking ties (when reputation is available) can sustain higher cooperation. However, because efficiency or group payoff is not measured, and the game is not a PGG, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The main implication is that information about others' behavior (reputation) and the ability to avoid defectors can substitute for explicit costly punishment in sustaining cooperation, but the effect on efficiency is not quantified.

- source: Jensen, K., Call, J., & Tomasello, M. (2007). Chimpanzees are rational maximizers in an ultimatum game. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Chimpanzees, when tested in a mini-ultimatum game, do not systematically reject unfair offers, even when a fair alternative is available to the proposer. Proposers do not make fair offers, and responders accept almost all nonzero offers, only reliably rejecting offers where they receive nothing. This contrasts with human behavior, where unfair offers are often rejected as a form of costly punishment. The findings suggest that chimpanzees are rational maximizers and do not engage in costly punishment to enforce fairness. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on behavioral responses to fairness and punishment opportunities.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides limited direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it studies a mini-ultimatum game with chimpanzees and focuses on behavioral outcomes (offer choices, rejection rates) rather than efficiency or group payoff. However, it is informative in showing that, in the absence of a willingness to punish unfairness (i.e., when punishment is not exercised), there is no mechanism to enforce fair or cooperative outcomes. This suggests that, in environments where punishment is not used or is not costly, efficiency gains from punishment are unlikely. The findings are adjacent to the prediction task and may inform models about the necessity of actual punishment behavior for efficiency effects.

- source: Zhen, S. S., & Yu, R. J. (2016). Tend to Compare and Tend to Be Fair: The Relationship between Social Comparison Sensitivity and Justice Sensitivity. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds a positive correlation between social comparison sensitivity and justice sensitivity, both in behavioral tasks (UG and SCG) and in self-report inventories. In the UG, participants are more likely to reject disadvantageous offers, and this tendency is associated with their behavior in the SCG. The results suggest that social comparison motives underlie fairness-related decisions, particularly the rejection of unfair offers. However, the study does not include a public goods game, does not manipulate or analyze punishment as a separate stage, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It uses a modified ultimatum game (dyadic, proposer-responder) and a social comparison game, focusing on individual differences in fairness and comparison sensitivity. There is no group interaction, no punishment stage as in PGGs, and no efficiency or group payoff data. The findings may be relevant for understanding motives behind rejection (which can be interpreted as a form of costly punishment), but the lack of group-level payoff outcomes and absence of a PGG or punishment-enabled/control design means this paper offers only indirect, limited support for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Mancini, A., Betti, V., Panasiti, M. S., Pavone, E. F., & Aglioti, S. M. (2011). Suffering Makes You Egoist: Acute Pain Increases Acceptance Rates and Reduces Fairness during a Bilateral Ultimatum Game. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that acute pain leads to higher acceptance rates of unfair offers and lower fairness in offers made, suggesting that pain induces more self-interested behavior in both roles of the Ultimatum Game. The effect is specific to pain (not just heat) and is most pronounced for moderately unfair offers. The intensity of pain predicts acceptance rates for these offers. However, the study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or any direct payoff-based outcomes, and does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It uses the Ultimatum Game, which is adjacent but not equivalent to PGGs, and focuses on the effect of pain (a state manipulation) rather than institutional punishment. The outcomes are behavioral (acceptance, offer size) rather than payoff-based. Thus, its relevance for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like games is limited.

- source: Han, X., Cao, S. N., Shen, Z. S., Zhang, B. Y., Wang, W. X., Cressman, R., & Stanley, H. E. (2017). Emergence of communities and diversity in social networks. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that in static network structures (both regular and random bipartite networks), proposers form stable local communities with diverse offers, while in randomly rewired networks, proposers become homogeneous. The diversity in offers (SD of p) is much higher in structured networks. Responders' behavior is heterogeneous but not affected by network structure. The mechanism is that rational proposers best-respond to their local responder 'leaders,' whose heterogeneous minimum acceptance levels drive local agreement. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or surplus, and does not include a public goods game or explicit costly punishment mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The experiment uses the ultimatum game, where 'punishment' is the rejection of offers, not a costly sanctioning mechanism. The main outcomes are behavioral (offer diversity, community formation), not payoff-based. Therefore, this paper should not be used as primary evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like games. It may provide some indirect context about how local interaction structure and heterogeneity can affect behavioral diversity, but not efficiency or payoff outcomes.

- source: Bengart, P., Gruendler, T., & Vogt, B. (2021). Acute tryptophan depletion in healthy subjects increases preferences for negative reciprocity. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that lowering serotonin via ATD increases negative reciprocity in the Hawk-Dove game: participants in the ATD group are more likely to choose Hawk in response to an expected Hawk, compared to placebo. This effect is specific to negative reciprocity and does not extend to positive reciprocity or beliefs about the opponent's action. The incentive for mutual cooperation (payoff for mutual Dove) reduces Hawk choices. Risk attitude and mood are unaffected by ATD and do not predict strategy choice. The study does not analyze or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that biological factors (serotonin availability) can modulate negative reciprocity in a competitive, dyadic game, but it does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment mechanisms or sanctions in public goods or similar games. The Hawk-Dove game is structurally adjacent to public goods and punishment games, but lacks explicit punishment or sanctioning stages, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Therefore, the findings are only weakly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The main relevance is in showing that negative reciprocity (costly, non-payoff-maximizing responses to perceived unkindness) can be increased by serotonin depletion, but the translation to efficiency or group payoff in multi-player, repeated, or sanction-enabled games is limited.

- source: Ma, L. K., Tunney, R. J., & Ferguson, E. (2014). Gratefully Received, Gratefully Repaid: The Role of Perceived Fairness in Cooperative Interactions. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that recipients of unconditional (free) help perceive the donor as more genuinely helpful, feel more gratitude, and report a higher tendency to reciprocate in the future compared to recipients of conditional (repayment-required) help. Among those who received conditional offers and accepted, 38% defaulted (either partially or fully) on repayment, suggesting a behavioral response to perceived unfairness. The effect of offer conditionality on willingness to reciprocate is serially mediated by perceived helpfulness and gratitude. The study does not report on group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, and the game is a one-shot, dyadic trust game rather than a repeated public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how conditionality (akin to punishment or sanctions) affects willingness to reciprocate and actual repayment behavior in a one-shot, dyadic trust game. It does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The findings suggest that perceived fairness and the framing of help (conditional vs. unconditional) can influence cooperation and defaulting, which may be relevant for understanding psychological mechanisms in cooperation, but the results cannot be directly used to predict efficiency changes in public goods games with punishment enabled or disabled.

- source: Frey, V., & de Rijt, A. V. (2016). Arbitrary Inequality in Reputation Systems. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when trustors can observe the outcomes of others' exchanges (information sharing), they are more likely to repeatedly select the same trustee, leading to 'reputation cascades' and increasing inequality in how often trustees are chosen. This effect is strongest when there is a risk of trust abuse (the 'trust problem' condition). The paper shows that these cascades are arbitrary, as trustees with similar trustworthiness can end up with very different exchange volumes. While the paper discusses payoffs in the game structure, it does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes. The main focus is on behavioral dynamics of trust and reputation, not on efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of information sharing and reputation systems on behavioral outcomes in trust-based exchange games. It does not directly inform predictions about efficiency or group payoff in public goods or punishment games, as it does not report these outcomes. However, it suggests that increased information sharing can increase trust and exchange, but also leads to arbitrary inequality in participation. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in public goods games with punishment, this paper is only weakly informative, as the game structure and outcomes differ from standard PGGs and do not include punishment or efficiency as primary outcomes.

- source: Bshary, R., & Grutter, A. S. (2006). Image scoring and cooperation in a cleaner fish mutualism. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: show_other_summaries
  findings: The study shows that client fish preferentially associate with cleaner fish that are observed to behave cooperatively, providing evidence for image scoring in a non-human animal. Cleaner fish, in turn, behave more cooperatively (feed against their preference) when their actions are being observed by a potential client (image-scoring scenario) compared to when they are not observed. This supports the hypothesis that reputation-based partner choice can promote cooperation. However, the study does not measure group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and the experimental setup is not a public goods game but an asymmetric mutualism with partner choice.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reputation mechanisms (image scoring) can increase cooperative behavior in asymmetric mutualisms, which may be relevant for understanding the effects of reputation or partner choice in public goods games. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or their close variants. The findings may inform predictions about the role of observation and reputation in promoting cooperation, but not about the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Yamamoto, H., Okada, I., & Suzuki, T. (2025). Gradual reputation dynamics evolve and sustain cooperation in indirect reciprocity. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: The experimental results show that people use a three-level (good, neutral, bad) reputation system and update reputations gradually: cooperation increases reputation by one level, defection decreases it by one, except that defection against a bad recipient (justified defection) leaves reputation unchanged. Theoretical evolutionary game modeling demonstrates that, under certain conditions, tolerant norms that cooperate with good and neutral and defect only against bad can dominate and sustain high cooperation rates. However, the study does not use a public goods game or measure group efficiency or payoff; it focuses on reputation dynamics and the evolution of cooperation in indirect reciprocity.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its findings are adjacent: it informs how nuanced reputation and justified defection (a form of social sanction) are perceived and how these perceptions can support cooperation in indirect reciprocity. However, it does not manipulate or measure punishment costs, punishment effectiveness, or group payoffs, nor does it use a PGG or similar economic game. Thus, it should be used only as indirect, contextual evidence about the psychological and evolutionary underpinnings of social sanctions and cooperation.

- source: Bshary, R., Grutter, A. S., Willener, A. S. T., & Leimar, O. (2008). Pairs of cooperating cleaner fish provide better service quality than singletons. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: Pairs of cleaner fish provide higher service quality to clients than singletons, mainly because females behave more cooperatively in pairs. Theoretical modeling shows that cooperation in pairs can yield higher service quality if there is added search efficiency, and empirical data (field and lab) support this. Aggression by males toward cheating females may function as a form of punishment, but this is not systematically manipulated or measured as a treatment. The outcomes are behavioral (cheating, cooperation, aggression), not direct measures of efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It shows that, in a biological system with prisoner's dilemma-like structure, the presence of a partner (pair vs singleton) can increase cooperative behavior and service quality, especially when there is some mechanism for partner control (e.g., aggression/punishment). However, the study does not use a public goods game, does not manipulate punishment as a treatment, and does not report efficiency or group payoff. Thus, it is only adjacent to the prediction task and should be used as weak, indirect support for the idea that social context and partner control can moderate cooperation and service quality.

- source: Wiessner, P. (2020). The role of third parties in norm enforcement in customary courts among the Enga of Papua New Guinea. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The study documents that in Enga customary courts, norm violations are addressed primarily through restorative justice: compensation to victims, reintegration of wrongdoers, and broad third-party participation in settlements. Punitive sanctions (punishment) are rare and not institutionally emphasized. Third parties contribute to compensation both during and outside court hearings, motivated by kinship, reputation, and community harmony. Repeated offenders and free riders receive less support over time. The system is designed to maintain cooperation and social cohesion, not to maximize punishment or deterrence. The paper argues that such restorative systems are more effective for sustaining cooperation in small-scale societies than third-party punishment, but does not provide quantitative efficiency or payoff data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides contextual and ethnographic evidence that, in small-scale societies like the Enga, restorative justice and third-party compensation are preferred over punitive sanctions for norm enforcement. While it does not provide quantitative data on efficiency or group payoff, it suggests that punishment is not the primary or most effective means of sustaining cooperation in such environments. For prediction tasks involving public goods games or efficiency outcomes, this paper is mainly informative for understanding the limits of punishment's effectiveness and the importance of alternative, restorative mechanisms. It does not provide direct evidence for how punishment affects efficiency in public goods games or similar experimental settings.

- source: Kandori, M., & Obayashi, S. (2014). Labor union members play an OLG repeated game. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that cooperation (helping behavior) among union members is sustained not by explicit punishment, perfect monitoring, or reputation, but by a focal-point equilibrium where members help others in expectation of reciprocal help when they need it. Most members leave the union after their own disputes are resolved, indicating that helping is conditional and self-interested rather than purely altruistic. The equilibrium is supported by direct observation and mutual monitoring during joint activities, but not by formal sanctions or payoff-based incentives. The paper provides qualitative evidence and a theoretical model, but does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar laboratory environments. It provides qualitative, contextual evidence about how cooperation can be sustained in a real-world OLG setting with limited observability, but does not report or manipulate efficiency, payoff, or formal punishment mechanisms. The findings may be useful for understanding the range of mechanisms that can support cooperation in loosely knit groups, but do not provide quantitative or experimental evidence relevant to the prediction task.

- source: Pisor, A. C., Gervais, M. M., Purzycki, B. G., & Ross, C. T. (2020). Preferences and constraints: the value of economic games for studying human behaviour. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, reward_exists, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that economic games can be strategically designed to reveal either private-world or real-world preferences, depending on which constraints are relaxed (e.g., resource constraints, anonymity, framing). In the Colombian case study, allocation and punishment behaviors in the games map onto real-world social variables (e.g., kinship, ethnicity, need), but also reveal preferences that are masked in observational data due to real-world constraints. Costly punishment (costly reduction) is more likely to be directed at wealthy or high-status individuals, and less likely at marginalized individuals, but the paper does not analyze the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff. The main contribution is methodological: showing how game design choices affect what is measured and how results relate to real-world behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. While it discusses costly punishment (costly reduction) games, the outcomes are about who is punished and why, not about group payoff or efficiency. The findings are useful for understanding how punishment behavior is shaped by social context and game design, but do not inform the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency. The paper is most useful for methodological considerations and for understanding the importance of matching game design to research questions and local context.

- source: HEINSOHN, R., & PACKER, C. (1995). COMPLEX COOPERATIVE STRATEGIES IN GROUP-TERRITORIAL AFRICAN LIONS. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The study finds that female lions show consistent individual differences in their willingness to participate in group territorial defense, with some acting as leaders and others as laggards. Laggards are recognized by leaders, who become more cautious in their presence but do not punish them or withdraw cooperation. Some laggards adjust their participation based on the odds of group success, while others do not. The diversity of strategies suggests that cooperation in large groups is more complex than predicted by simple reciprocity or punishment models. No evidence of punishment or sanctioning of laggards is found.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the maintenance of cooperation in group settings without formal punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. It suggests that in naturalistic, high-stakes group conflicts, cooperation can persist with a mix of unconditional and conditional cooperators and laggards, and that punishment is not always used to enforce cooperation. However, because the study does not use a public goods game or measure efficiency/payoff outcomes, it offers only adjacent, qualitative insight for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments. It highlights the possibility that cooperation can be stable without punishment, but does not provide quantitative or design-specific evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Kümmerli, R. (2011). A Test of Evolutionary Policing Theory with Data from Human Societies. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that (1) higher similarity among citizens is associated with lower crime rates (interpreted as higher cooperation), (2) lower similarity is associated with higher policing effort, and (3) increases in policing effort over time are associated with reductions in crime rates. These findings support evolutionary policing theory in a real-world context. However, the outcomes are not directly efficiency or payoff-based, but rather use crime rate as a behavioral proxy for defection/cooperation. The study does not use a public goods game or experimental design, and does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence that increased punishment (policing) can reduce defection (crime) in real-world settings, especially in less homogeneous populations. However, because it does not use a public goods game, does not manipulate punishment experimentally, and does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, it is of limited direct use for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. Its main value is in supporting the general plausibility of punishment reducing defection, but it does not inform the quantitative relationship between game design dimensions and efficiency.

- source: Ge, E. H., Chen, Y., Wu, J. J., & Mace, R. (2019). Large-scale cooperation driven by reputation, not fear of divine punishment. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that cooperation (measured as donations) is higher in small, stable communities and among those with more public religious practice, but not among those with stronger belief in divine punishment. When reputational concerns are removed (private dice game), neither community size nor religiosity predicts honesty. The results suggest that reputation, not fear of supernatural punishment, drives large-scale cooperation in these settings. No evidence is provided on the effects of punishment or sanctions, nor on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The experimental design does not include punishment, sanctions, or group payoff/efficiency measures. Its findings are only indirectly relevant: they suggest that reputational mechanisms (public observability, community stability) can increase prosocial behavior, but do not address the impact of punishment or efficiency. Thus, it should not be used as primary evidence for the downstream prediction task, except as very weak contextual support for the importance of reputation versus punishment.

- source: Handley, C., & Mathew, S. (2020). Human large-scale cooperation as a product of competition between cultural groups. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds a strong negative association between cultural differentiation (measured by Fst across 49 norms) and the prevalence of cooperative norms toward outgroup members. Geographic distance does not significantly predict cooperation once cultural differentiation is controlled for. The effect is strongest for norms related to raiding/warfare. The findings support the idea that group-level selection on cultural variation shapes the scale of cooperative norms, but the study does not involve economic games, payoff measurement, or experimental manipulation of punishment or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not use a PGG or variant, does not manipulate or measure punishment as a treatment, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Its findings are relevant for understanding the cultural evolution of cooperation and the role of norm enforcement in real-world societies, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in controlled game-theoretic settings.

- source: Dawes, C. T., Fowler, J. H., Johnson, T., McElreath, R., & Smirnov, O. (2007). Egalitarian motives in humans. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that people are willing to pay to reduce or augment others' incomes even when there is no cooperative norm to enforce and no material gain from doing so. Both costly taking (punishment-like) and costly giving (reward-like) are common, and are strongly targeted by relative income: high earners are penalized, low earners are rewarded. Negative emotions towards high earners increase with inequality and predict more costly taking and giving. The design randomizes group composition and income, ensuring that actions cannot be motivated by reciprocity or reputation. The study provides evidence for egalitarian motives in income-altering behavior, but does not address efficiency or payoff outcomes in a public goods or cooperation context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments, as it does not include a contribution/cooperation stage or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The design is adjacent to PGGs with punishment/reward, but isolates egalitarian motives by removing cooperation. The findings suggest that punishment-like and reward-like behaviors can be motivated by inequality aversion and negative emotions, not just norm enforcement. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in PGGs, this paper provides only indirect, contextual evidence about the psychological motives behind punishment/reward, not about their effect on efficiency.

- source: Ma, N., Li, N., He, X. S., Sun, D. L., Zhang, X. C., & Zhang, D. R. (2012). Rejection of Unfair Offers Can Be Driven by Negative Emotions, Evidence from Modified Ultimatum Games with Anonymity. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that rejection rates of unfair offers are significantly above zero even when anonymity is strictly enforced, suggesting that negative emotions alone can drive costly punishment-like behavior. Rejection rates are higher in public (experimenter-observed) or 'informed' conditions, indicating that reputational concerns and cognitive processing of consequences further increase rejection. However, the study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and the game is a modified ultimatum game rather than a public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its main contribution is to clarify the psychological motivations for costly punishment (rejection) in ultimatum-like games, distinguishing between emotional and reputational drivers. It does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so it cannot be used to calibrate the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like settings. It may be useful for understanding the psychological underpinnings of punishment behavior, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Yamagishi, T., Horita, Y., Mifune, N., Hashimoto, H., Li, Y., Shinada, M., Miura, A., Inukai, K., Takagishi, H., & Simunovic, D. (2012). Rejection of unfair offers in the ultimatum game is no evidence of strong reciprocity. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The main finding is that individuals who reject unfair offers in the ultimatum game do not show greater prosociality or positive reciprocity in other economic games (dictator, trust, or prisoner's dilemma). The rejection of unfair offers is not correlated with prosocial value orientation or reciprocity scales, but is positively correlated with assertiveness. The results challenge the interpretation of UG rejection as evidence of strong reciprocity and suggest it may instead reflect a desire to avoid inferior status. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not use a public goods game or a punishment mechanism analogous to those in PGG experiments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The study is focused on the ultimatum game, which is structurally different from PGGs, and examines rejection (a form of costly punishment) as a behavioral outcome, not its effect on group efficiency or payoff. The findings suggest that rejection in the UG may not be motivated by the same prosocial or norm-enforcing preferences that drive punishment in PGGs. Therefore, the results caution against interpreting UG rejection as evidence for the effectiveness or motivation of punishment in PGG-like settings, but do not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence about efficiency outcomes under punishment.

- source: de Melo, C. M., Terada, K., & Santos, F. C. (2021). Emotion expressions shape human social norms and reputations. *Iscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that participants cooperate more with counterparts who have positive or unknown reputations, use generous strategies, and display cooperative emotion expressions. Reputation, strategy, and emotion all independently and interactively affect cooperation rates and how reputations are updated. Emotion expressions can help forgive defectors with good reputations or rehabilitate those with bad reputations if they behave generously and express cooperative emotions. However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or any direct payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It uses an iterated prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, and focuses on cooperation rates and reputation rather than efficiency or group payoff. There is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the sense of costly punishment; instead, the study manipulates emotion expressions and reputation. Thus, its relevance for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like games is limited. It may be useful for understanding how nonverbal cues and reputation affect cooperation, but not for payoff-based prediction tasks.

- source: Singh, M., & Garfield, Z. H. (2022). Evidence for third-party mediation but not punishment in Mentawai justice. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that, in the Mentawai context, third-party punishment is absent: no cases were found where third parties (outside the victim, their close kin, or spouse) directly punished or demanded penalties from transgressors. Indirect or second-order sanctions by third parties are also not observed. Instead, penalties (tulou) are negotiated and paid primarily to restore dyadic cooperation, with kinship playing a major role in the likelihood and size of payments. Mediation by third parties (especially shamans and elders) is common, particularly in more severe or between-clan disputes, but the mediator's role is to facilitate negotiation, not to enforce punishment. The study suggests that, in this small-scale society, institutionalized penalties serve to repair relationships rather than to enforce group-level norms through punishment. The findings are based on retrospective interviews and ethnographic observation of 302 reliable cases of transgression and response.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The studied context is not a PGG or close variant, and the outcomes are not efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that, in small-scale, kin-based societies, third-party punishment is rare or absent, and penalties serve to restore dyadic cooperation rather than to enforce group norms or increase group efficiency. If extrapolating to PGG-like settings, the results would suggest that the presence or absence of third-party punishment may depend on the social structure and the nature of cooperation (private vs. public goods), but the paper does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency outcomes. Thus, it is of limited use for the downstream prediction task, except as a caution that punishment mechanisms may not always function as assumed in small-scale, kin-based societies.

- source: Yamamoto, H., & Suzuki, T. (2025). Exploring condition in which people accept AI over human judgements on justified defection. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The experiments show that participants are generally neutral in judging justified defection (not helping a person with a bad reputation). When asked to accept the judgement of a manager (AI or human) who rates the defector as good or bad, participants' acceptance depends on the combination: they only prefer the AI's positive judgement over a human's negative judgement. In all other cases, acceptance is neutral. The study does not measure or report any efficiency, payoff, or group welfare outcomes, nor does it manipulate or analyze game-theoretic parameters such as MPCR, punishment cost, or group size.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods or indirect reciprocity games. It does not report or analyze any payoff-based outcomes, nor does it manipulate or report on game design dimensions such as MPCR, punishment cost, or group size. Its findings are limited to attitudinal acceptance of AI versus human judgements in a scenario of justified defection. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about efficiency or payoff effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Wallace, B., Cesarini, D., Lichtenstein, P., & Johannesson, M. (2007). Heritability of ultimatum game responder behavior. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The main finding is that individual differences in the willingness to reject unfair offers in the ultimatum game are substantially heritable, with additive genetic effects explaining about 42% of the variance in acceptance thresholds. The study uses a large sample of monozygotic and dizygotic twins from the Swedish Twin Registry and applies standard structural equation modeling. There is no evidence that common environment plays a significant role. The results are robust across sexes. The paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment institutions, and does not use a public goods game or a repeated game structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its relevance is limited to showing that individual differences in costly punishment behavior (as measured by ultimatum game rejections) have a substantial genetic component. It does not manipulate punishment institutions, does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, and does not use a public goods or repeated game design. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments with or without punishment.

- source: Jensen, K., Call, J., & Tomasello, M. (2007). Chimpanzees are vengeful but not spiteful. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: Chimpanzees do not engage in 'spiteful' punishment (i.e., incurring a cost to reduce another's payoff in response to disadvantageous outcomes alone), but do engage in 'vengeful' punishment (i.e., retaliating when a conspecific actively steals food from them). The tendency to retaliate declines over repeated sessions, and is not affected by kinship or prior reciprocity. There is no evidence that such punishment-like behavior enforces cooperation or increases group welfare, as stealing increases over time and retaliation declines. The study does not use a public goods game or measure efficiency, but provides evidence about the motivations underlying punishment-like behaviors in chimpanzees.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments, as it does not use a PGG or measure efficiency, group payoff, or welfare. However, it is informative for understanding the motivational basis of punishment: chimpanzees retaliate against harmful actions but do not punish mere outcome inequality. This suggests that, in species or contexts where punishment is only vengeful and not spiteful, punishment may not stabilize cooperation or improve efficiency as it does in human PGGs. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of punishment mechanisms alone is not sufficient for efficiency gains; the underlying motivation and context (e.g., response to intentional harm vs. outcome disparity) matter. However, the lack of payoff outcomes and the non-PGG design limit its direct predictive value.

- source: Lin, C. A., & Bates, T. C. (2022). Free to choose: Mutualist motives for partner choice, proportional division, punishment, and help. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that mutualism is a reliable, stable, and discriminant psychological construct, with four components: partner choice, proportional sharing, proportional punishment, and proportional helping. Higher mutualism scores predict attitudinal opposition to equal sharing and redistribution, and preference for proportional division in hypothetical scenarios. The mutualism scale is largely independent of compassion, envy, self-interest, and utilitarianism. The findings are robust across two large samples. However, all outcomes are attitudinal or psychometric; there is no evidence on actual behavior in public goods games, punishment games, or on efficiency/welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It may be useful for understanding individual differences in motives that could influence behavior in such games, but it does not report any behavioral or payoff-based outcomes, nor does it manipulate or observe game design dimensions. Thus, it should not be used as primary evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment interventions. Its main value is in providing a validated measure of mutualist motives, which could be used in future studies that do measure relevant outcomes.

- source: Basu, S., Dickhaut, J., Hecht, G., Towry, K., & Waymire, G. (2009). Recordkeeping alters economic history by promoting reciprocity. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Allowing subjects to keep records in a repeated multi-dyad trust game leads to higher mean investor returns (18.7% vs. 7.3% in periods 6-10) and lower risk (coefficient of variation 0.221 vs. 0.358) compared to no-recordkeeping conditions. Recordkeeping promotes reputation formation, more coordinated reciprocal behavior, and greater discrimination between trustworthy and untrustworthy partners. The effect is especially pronounced in later rounds. The study provides direct evidence that external memory (recordkeeping) can beneficially alter economic history by increasing efficiency and reducing risk in complex exchange environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that informational institutions like recordkeeping can substantially increase efficiency and reduce risk in repeated trust-based exchange, especially in complex (multi-partner) settings. While it does not study punishment, it demonstrates that mechanisms which improve memory and reputation tracking can have large positive effects on efficiency. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that enabling information storage or reputation tracking (even without explicit punishment) can boost efficiency, particularly as the number of partners increases and over repeated rounds. The results are most directly relevant to settings where the main challenge is memory and reputation, not norm enforcement via punishment.

- source: Ibbotson, P., Hauert, C., & Walker, R. (2019). Effort Perception is Made More Accurate with More Effort and When Cooperating with Slackers. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that participants are generally accurate in perceiving their partner's effort in a cooperative task, with higher accuracy when the partner is a slacker and when the participant invests more effort. The experimental design involves a two-player online game where both invest effort to move balls into a bucket, with rewards split 50/50. The main outcome is the accuracy of effort perception, not efficiency or payoff. Theoretical modeling suggests that strategies based on effort perception can help avoid inefficient cooperation, but this is not empirically tested in terms of group payoff or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes. The findings are relevant to understanding how effort perception may support cooperation, but do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment or game design dimensions. The theoretical discussion of effort-prober strategies is adjacent to efficiency but not empirically linked to payoff outcomes.

- source: Knoch, D., Schneider, F., Schunk, D., Hohmann, M., & Fehr, E. (2009). Disrupting the prefrontal cortex diminishes the human ability to build a good reputation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that when trustees know their past actions will be visible to future partners (reputation condition), they return significantly more to investors than in the anonymous condition. Disrupting the right DLPFC with rTMS eliminates this effect, causing trustees to behave as if reputation incentives are absent. The effect is strongest when the temptation to defect is highest (i.e., when the investor sends the maximum amount). The study provides causal evidence that the right DLPFC is necessary for overcoming short-term temptations to build a good reputation. However, the paper does not include a punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and efficiency or group payoff is not the primary outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is only weakly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The design is a repeated trust game with reputation incentives, not a public goods game, and there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism. The main behavioral outcome is back-transfer rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The findings may be indirectly informative about the role of reputation and self-control in sustaining cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence about punishment effects or efficiency outcomes. Thus, this paper should not be weighted heavily for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Francey, D., & Bergmüller, R. (2012). Images of Eyes Enhance Investments in a Real-Life Public Good. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Images of eyes, as subtle cues of being watched, increased the amount of time and effort people invested in removing garbage from public bus stop benches, but did not affect the likelihood that people would engage in garbage removal at all. The effect was not due to increased attention to a sign requesting garbage removal. The study provides field evidence that reputational cues can enhance non-monetary investments in public goods, but does not address efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it involve punishment or sanction mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not manipulate or measure punishment, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Its main relevance is as indirect evidence that reputational cues (such as being watched) can increase cooperative investment in real-world public good contexts, but this is not mediated by punishment or efficiency. It should not be used as primary evidence for the prediction task, but may provide weak contextual support for the importance of reputational mechanisms.

  findings: The study finds that instructing participants to make intuitive decisions (as opposed to reflective or control) increases the amount of reward given to prosocial allocators in a third-party reward task. Honesty-Humility, a personality trait, is not significantly associated with reward behavior. The effect of intuition is robust to controls for other personality dimensions and social value orientation.
