- source: Blair, G., Littman, R., & Paluck, E. L. (2019). Motivating the adoption of new community-minded behaviors: An empirical test in Nigeria. *Science Advances*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study found that both a social norms intervention (film with role models) and a nudge intervention (mass text message) increased the number of people who reported corruption by text message in Nigerian communities. The mass text message had a much larger and more immediate effect than the film. The film shifted some perceptions of social norms (e.g., increased perceived prevalence of corruption and anger about corruption), but did not increase the perceived prevalence of reporting. There was no analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or any payoff-based outcome, and no punishment or sanction mechanism was present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The experimental context is adjacent to public goods (encouraging costly community-minded behavior), but there is no group payoff, no efficiency measure, and no punishment or sanctioning mechanism. The findings may be of limited conceptual interest for understanding the adoption of rare cooperative behaviors, but do not inform the prediction task regarding punishment and efficiency.

- source: Liu, G. F., Lin, C. D., & Xin, Z. Q. (2014). The Effects of Within- and Between-Group Competition on Trust and Trustworthiness among Acquaintances. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that both within-group and between-group competition increase trust (investment) among acquaintances in an investment game. However, within-group competition reduces trustworthiness (return ratio) and leads to lower payoffs for trustors but higher payoffs for trustees. Between-group competition increases trustee payoffs but does not significantly affect trustworthiness or trustor payoffs compared to the non-competition condition. The results suggest that competition type differentially affects efficiency for trustors and trustees, and that within-group competition can undermine overall group efficiency by harming trustor payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in the absence of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, introducing within-group competition can increase trust but reduce trustworthiness, leading to lower payoffs for trustors and higher payoffs for trustees. Between-group competition increases trust and trustee payoffs but does not harm trustor payoffs. Since the study does not include punishment, it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does show that competitive structures can have asymmetric effects on efficiency depending on the role. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that the presence or absence of competition (and its type) can moderate efficiency outcomes, but the absence of punishment means the results are not directly transferable to punishment-enabled PGGs.

- source: Johnson, T., & Obradovich, N. (2025). Testing for completions that simulate altruism in early language models. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study demonstrates that certain LLMs (notably text-davinci-003 and GPT-4) generate completions that simulate both payoff-maximizing behavior in non-social tasks and altruistic sharing in dictator game scenarios. The simulated sharing rates for text-davinci-003 are close to those observed in human dictator game experiments, while less advanced models do not consistently maximize payoffs or share resources. The findings are robust to prompt variations, model parameters, and resource framing. No punishment, sanctioning, or public goods game (PGG) structure is present; the focus is on one-shot dictator games and non-social maximization tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does, however, offer high-quality evidence on LLMs' simulated behavior in dictator games and non-social maximization tasks, including payoff-based outcomes. Since there is no punishment or sanctioning, and the game is not a PGG or close variant, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments. It may be useful for understanding LLMs' simulated social preferences or for comparison with human dictator game data, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Efferson, C., Lalive, R., & Fehr, E. (2008). The coevolution of cultural groups and ingroup favoritism. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a repeated two-player coordination game with two equilibria, allowing players to freely choose and maintain symbolic markers (shapes) leads to the endogenous formation of 'cultural groups' where markers become predictive of behavior. This results in strong ingroup favoritism (preference for same-marker partners), increased coordination on the optimal behavior, and significantly higher mean payoffs compared to a control where markers are randomized and cannot acquire meaning. When the payoff structure removes unobservable heterogeneity or markers are fixed, the effect disappears. The findings show that endogenous group formation via symbolic markers can substantially increase efficiency in coordination games, but only under specific conditions (marker mutability and persistent heterogeneity). No punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in coordination games with multiple equilibria and persistent unobservable heterogeneity, allowing players to endogenously form groups via flexible symbolic markers can increase efficiency (mean payoff) by enabling better coordination. However, the design does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The results are most relevant for understanding how group markers and endogenous assortment affect efficiency, not punishment. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly informative, but it does highlight the importance of group structure and endogenous assortment for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Wang, Z., Jusup, M., Shi, L., Lee, J. H., Iwasa, Y., & Boccaletti, S. (2018). Exploiting a cognitive bias promotes cooperation in social dilemma experiments. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: Introducing a decoy 'reward' option in a repeated prisoner's dilemma increases cooperation rates and improves the likelihood that cooperators achieve higher payoffs. The decoy is rarely chosen, but its presence shifts initial choices toward cooperation, and this effect is stable over time. In the control, higher cooperation is associated with lower payoffs, but in the decoy condition, this negative correlation disappears, suggesting that cooperation is no longer disadvantageous in terms of payoff. The effect is robust to gender and academic background. The study does not involve punishment or sanctions, but rather a prosocial decoy option.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not study punishment or public goods games directly, but it provides evidence that introducing a prosocial decoy option can increase cooperation and improve the payoff prospects for cooperators in repeated dyadic social dilemmas. For prediction tasks involving efficiency, the findings suggest that adding a third, prosocial but inferior option can shift behavior and payoffs, even if the option is rarely chosen. However, the results do not directly inform the effect of punishment or sanctions, nor do they address group public goods settings. The payoff analysis is relevant for understanding how behavioral interventions (like decoys) can affect efficiency, but the lack of punishment and the dyadic structure limit direct transfer to PGG-punishment environments.

- source: Chiang, Y. S. (2015). Good Samaritans in Networks: An Experiment on How Networks Influence Egalitarian Sharing and the Evolution of Inequality. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that voluntary sharing in networks can reduce inequality, but the extent depends on network topology. When networks connect the very rich and very poor (Lattice_Hetero, SF_Negative), local inequality is salient and motivates more redistribution, leading to significant reductions in the Gini coefficient. In other network structures (Full, Lattice_Homo, SF_Positive), inequality is not significantly reduced. The study does not involve public goods provision, punishment, or sanctioning, and does not report efficiency as the ratio of group payoff to the fully cooperative benchmark. The findings are most relevant to how network structure moderates voluntary redistribution and the evolution of inequality.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency in the sense required for the downstream prediction task. The findings are about voluntary redistribution and inequality in networked settings, which is adjacent but not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The paper may provide some contextual insight into how network structure affects redistribution, but it does not inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency in PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Drouvelis, M., Gerson, J., Powdthavee, N., & Riyanto, Y. E. (2023). Large losses from little lies: Strategic gender misrepresentation and cooperation. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that when participants are randomly allowed to misrepresent their gender to their partner in a one-shot Golden Balls game, the average defection (steal) rate increases by about 10-12 percentage points compared to control conditions (blind or true gender revealed). Those who actually choose to misrepresent are about 32 percentage points more likely to defect. The possibility of misrepresentation also increases defection among those who do not misrepresent, due to increased distrust. The result is a substantial reduction in overall cooperation and group welfare, as measured by the proportion of participants receiving the maximum possible payoff (£10 each for mutual cooperation) and the average payment per participant. The effect is robust across lab and online samples, and is not explained by differences in personality traits or risk/trust attitudes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing opportunities for strategic misrepresentation of identity (here, gender) in a one-shot social dilemma with communication reduces cooperation and group welfare, even in the absence of explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. While not a public goods game, the Golden Balls game is a close variant of the prisoner's dilemma, and the findings are likely to generalize to other one-shot or anonymous social dilemmas with communication. The results suggest that any design feature that increases uncertainty about partner identity or intentions (such as allowing misrepresentation) can reduce efficiency. For the prediction task, this paper supports the expectation that enabling deception or increasing uncertainty will lower efficiency, even if punishment is not present. However, it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is included in the design.

- source: Grujic, J., Gracia-Lázaro, C., Milinski, M., Semmann, D., Traulsen, A., Cuesta, J. A., Moreno, Y., & Sánchez, A. (2014). A comparative analysis of spatial Prisoner's Dilemma experiments: Conditional cooperation and payoff irrelevance. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The main findings are: (1) In spatial Prisoner's Dilemma experiments on lattices, cooperation rates start high but quickly decline to low, nonzero levels (15-35%). (2) Defectors consistently earn more than cooperators, with a significant negative correlation between cooperation and earnings. (3) The behavioral rule most consistent with the data is moody conditional cooperation, where players' probability to cooperate depends on their own previous action and the number of cooperating neighbors. (4) There is no evidence that spatial structure (network topology, number of neighbors, or system size) promotes higher cooperation or changes the behavioral rule. (5) Payoff-based imitation rules (imitate-the-best, Fermi rule) do not explain the observed behavior. (6) The paper does not study punishment, rewards, or sanctions, and does not report group efficiency relative to the fully cooperative outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. It does, however, provide indirect evidence that in the absence of punishment, cooperation rates and group payoffs remain low in spatial Prisoner's Dilemma games, and that spatial structure alone does not increase efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper may help establish a baseline for control (no-punishment) efficiency in spatial PD-like environments, but offers no information about the effect of enabling punishment. The behavioral findings (moody conditional cooperation) may inform models of baseline behavior in the absence of incentives.

- source: Nettle, D., Colléony, A., & Cockerill, M. (2011). Variation in Cooperative Behaviour within a Single City. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds dramatic differences in cooperative behavior between two neighborhoods within the same city, with the deprived neighborhood showing much lower generosity in the Dictator Game, lower social capital, and less willingness to engage in certain forms of anonymous helping. However, face-to-face helping rates did not differ. The Dictator Game allocations correlated with social capital and with some real-world cooperative behaviors at the community level. The study does not involve public goods games, punishment, or efficiency outcomes, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It uses the Dictator Game (a unilateral allocation task) rather than a multi-person social dilemma, and does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism. No efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes are reported. The findings are relevant to understanding variation in prosociality and cooperation across communities, but not to the prediction task focused on punishment effects in public goods games.

- source: Jordan, J. J., Hoffman, M., Nowak, M. A., & Rand, D. G. (2016). Uncalculating cooperation is used to signal trustworthiness. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiments demonstrate that uncalculating cooperation is used as a signal of trustworthiness: participants are more likely to cooperate without calculating costs when their decision process is observable to others, and such behavior leads to greater trust and actual trustworthiness in a subsequent trust game. The findings support a reputation-based account of uncalculating cooperation. However, the study does not address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency in public goods or similar group cooperation games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes. Its findings about reputation and uncalculating cooperation may be of theoretical interest for understanding motivations for cooperation, but do not inform the prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Blake, P. R., Rand, D. G., Tingley, D., & Warneken, F. (2015). The shadow of the future promotes cooperation in a repeated prisoner's dilemma for children. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Children aged 10-11 cooperate significantly more in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games than in one-shot games, demonstrating sensitivity to the 'shadow of the future' and the potential for direct reciprocity. Conditional cooperation strategies are observed, with girls and children with fewer conduct problems being more forgiving and cooperative. The study does not include any punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. It is relevant for understanding how repeated interaction (direct reciprocity) increases cooperation in dyadic games among children, but does not address punishment, sanctions, or payoff-based efficiency. Thus, it offers only indirect, contextual support for the prediction task, mainly by illustrating that repeated interaction alone (without punishment) can increase cooperation rates in dyadic settings.

- source: Yang, C. L., & Yue, C. S. J. (2019). Cooperation in an Assortative Matching Prisoners Dilemma Experiment with Pro-Social Dummies. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that assortative matching based on recent cooperation history can increase cooperation rates compared to random matching, but the effect is inconsistent across groups. Introducing pro-social dummies (always-cooperating computer players) into the assortative matching environment (WHc) leads to a substantial and robust increase in cooperation rates for all types of players, including those initially inclined to defect. The presence of dummies shifts the distribution of cooperation rates to higher values and reduces the prevalence of all-defection. Payoff is positively correlated with cooperation in the assortative matching treatments, but efficiency or group welfare is not directly measured or reported. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in any treatment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods or similar games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in any treatment. The findings are relevant for understanding the effects of assortative matching and exogenous pro-social agents on cooperation in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma, but not for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. The study does show that structural features (like matching rules and initial population composition) can have large effects on cooperation rates, which may be conceptually relevant for understanding moderators of cooperation, but it does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Harrison, F., Sciberras, J., & James, R. (2011). Strength of Social Tie Predicts Cooperative Investment in a Human Social Network. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that cooperative investment (measured as willingness to endure physical discomfort for another's benefit) is positively correlated with the strength of the social tie between donor and recipient, and is higher when the relationship is reciprocated. Participants sometimes invested more for close peers than for themselves. The study does not involve a public goods game or any punishment/sanction mechanism, nor does it analyze group-level efficiency or total payoff. The findings are relevant to understanding how social proximity and reciprocity affect individual-level cooperative behavior, but do not provide evidence about punishment or efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or group-level efficiency/payoff. Its findings about social tie strength and reciprocity may be contextually informative for understanding cooperation, but are not directly transferable to the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency. It should not be used as primary evidence for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Ma, S. M., Zhang, B. Y., Cao, S. N., Liu, J. S., & Wang, W. X. (2021). Limited memory optimizes cooperation in social dilemma experiments. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that providing players with memory of their own and their partner's actions from previous rounds (memory length L) in a repeated, randomized-pair Prisoner's Dilemma leads to a non-monotonic effect on cooperation: cooperation rates are lowest with no memory (L=0), increase sharply with minimal memory (L=1), and peak at intermediate memory (L=2), then decline with longer memory (L=3,4). The behavioral mechanism is memory-based conditional cooperation, where most subjects cooperate if their partner's recent cooperation rate is high. Theoretical models (replicator dynamics and Markov processes) fit the observed cooperation rates well. No punishment, reward, or efficiency outcomes are studied.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. It is relevant for understanding how memory and information about past actions affect cooperation rates in repeated dyadic social dilemmas, but it does not address group payoff, efficiency, or the impact of punishment or reward mechanisms. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that memory length (information about past actions) can have a non-monotonic effect on cooperation rates, but provides no evidence about efficiency or about punishment effects. It is not directly informative for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in public-goods-like environments.

- source: Romano, A., Balliet, D., Yamagishi, T., & Liu, J. H. (2017). Parochial trust and cooperation across 17 societies. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that people cooperate more with ingroup than outgroup members in a trust game, but reputational concern (common knowledge of group membership) increases cooperation with both ingroup and outgroup members. Men show slightly more ingroup favoritism than women, but social value orientation predicts general cooperation rather than parochialism. There is little cross-societal variation in ingroup favoritism, and this variation is not explained by material security, religiosity, or pathogen stress. The study does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it does not include punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes. It is relevant for understanding how reputational concerns (a form of indirect social incentive) can increase cooperation, but not for payoff-based or punishment-based interventions. Its design and findings are adjacent to the prediction task, offering only indirect context for how social information and group identity affect cooperation.

- source: Brodbeck, F. C., Kugler, K. G., Reif, J. A. M., & Maier, M. A. (2013). Morals Matter in Economic Games. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across four lab experiments, participants induced to adopt a Unity moral motive (via framing or subliminal priming) allocated more money to another participant in a one-shot risk-sharing game than those induced to adopt a Proportionality motive. This effect was only present in interpersonal (DSG) and not solitary (SIG) settings. The study does not use a public goods game or any punishment/sanction mechanism, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The findings are about the psychological determinants of solidarity behavior (unconditional giving) in risk-sharing contexts.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not manipulate or measure punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. However, it provides adjacent evidence that psychological framing (moral motives) can strongly affect other-regarding behavior in one-shot risk-sharing games. The design is not a PGG, and the outcome is not efficiency or group payoff, so its direct value for the prediction task is limited.

  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent

  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The study finds that in the absence of punishment or differential allocation of collective goods, individuals who contribute more to collective action gain reputational benefits and larger social support networks. These networks are associated with improved health outcomes for households. The results support the idea that reputational and network benefits can help sustain cooperation in collective action settings, even when free riding is not punished. However, the study does not measure or report group efficiency, total payoff, or surplus, and does not use a public goods game or experimental design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a real-world collective action setting without punishment or sanctions, reputational and social network benefits can sustain cooperation. However, it does not provide data on efficiency or payoff outcomes as defined in public goods games, nor does it manipulate or analyze punishment. Therefore, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its main relevance is as a case study of how cooperation can be maintained without punishment, via reputational and network mechanisms.

- source: Grujic, J., Eke, B., Cabrales, A., Cuesta, J. A., & Sánchez, A. (2012). Three is a crowd in iterated prisoner's dilemmas: experimental evidence on reciprocal behavior. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in iterated multiplayer Prisoner's Dilemma games, cooperation is sustained and even increases in two-player groups, but decays rapidly and remains low in groups of three or more. The main behavioral mechanism is 'moody conditional cooperation,' where players' likelihood to cooperate depends on both their own previous action and the number of cooperators in the previous round. There is substantial heterogeneity in initial generosity but less in reciprocity. The presence of more than two players leads to a sharp drop in sustained cooperation, suggesting that reciprocity alone is insufficient to maintain cooperation in larger groups without additional mechanisms such as punishment. No direct measures of efficiency or group payoff are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the absence of punishment or reward mechanisms, increasing group size from 2 to 3 or more sharply reduces sustained cooperation in iterated social dilemmas. For the downstream prediction task, it suggests that, without punishment, efficiency (if measured) would be high in two-player games and low in groups of three or more. However, because the study does not include punishment or efficiency outcomes, it cannot directly inform the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. It does, however, highlight the importance of group size as a moderator of cooperation and suggests that additional mechanisms (like punishment) may be necessary to sustain cooperation and efficiency in larger groups.

- source: Capraro, V., Smyth, C., Mylona, K., & Niblo, G. A. (2014). Benevolent Characteristics Promote Cooperative Behaviour among Humans. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that (1) benevolence—defined as unilaterally increasing another's payoff beyond one's own—exists in the population, (2) individuals who are more benevolent are more likely to cooperate in a subsequent one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma, and (3) priming participants to think benevolently increases their cooperation rate compared to priming malevolence. The study uses custom BT games and standard two-player Prisoner's Dilemma games, but does not include public goods games, punishment, or analyze efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its findings about benevolence and cooperation may be contextually relevant for understanding individual factors that promote cooperation, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like games.

- source: Molho, C., Soraperra, I., Schulz, J. F., & Shalvi, S. (2025). Guilt drives prosociality across 20 countries. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Across 20 countries, participants were more likely to make prosocial choices in dictator games when they had full information about the consequences of their actions (guilt condition) compared to when they could remain ignorant. Making choices observable to others (shame condition) had negligible or inconsistent effects on prosociality. Individual-level guilt-proneness predicted greater responsiveness to information, but country-level differences were not robust moderators. The study does not include punishment, sanctions, or public goods game structures, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include punishment, sanctions, or group interaction, and does not report efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes. Its findings on the effect of information and observability on individual prosocial choice in dictator games may be contextually informative for understanding mechanisms of prosociality, but are not directly transferable to the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency in PGG-like settings.

- source: Romano, A., Sutter, M., Liu, J. H., Yamagishi, T., & Balliet, D. (2021). National parochialism is ubiquitous across 42 nations around the world. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that people across 42 nations cooperate more with ingroup (same-nation) partners than with outgroup or unidentified partners in a one-shot, continuous prisoner's dilemma. This national parochialism is highly consistent across nations and is not explained by cultural distance, institutional quality, or ecological factors. Observability (public vs private) increases overall cooperation but does not moderate parochialism. There is substantial cross-national variation in baseline cooperation rates (impersonal cooperation), which is associated with cultural and ecological factors. No punishment, sanctioning, or efficiency outcomes are studied.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Its design (continuous PD, no punishment, no repeated interaction) is adjacent to but not directly informative for the downstream prediction task. The only potentially relevant dimension is the effect of observability (public vs private) on cooperation, but this is not tied to efficiency or payoff, and there is no punishment treatment.

- source: Everett, J. A. C., Faber, N. S., & Crockett, M. J. (2015). The influence of social preferences and reputational concerns on intergroup prosocial behaviour in gains and losses contexts. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that participants are more likely to help ingroup than outgroup members, and more likely to help when decisions are public (i.e., reputational concerns are present). However, ingroup favouritism is observed even in private (anonymous) conditions, suggesting a role for social preferences beyond reputation management. The effect of public/private decision setting is stronger in loss contexts than gain contexts, but the pattern of ingroup favouritism is similar in both. The cost/benefit (net expected value) of helping influences overall helping rates, but does not moderate the effect of group membership or decision setting. No efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its design is adjacent to PGGs in that it involves costly prosocial acts, but the outcome is individual helping rather than collective action. The findings may be relevant for understanding the role of social preferences and reputational concerns in prosocial behavior, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. Therefore, this paper should not be weighted heavily for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Jones, M. I., & Christakis, N. A. (2024). Bringing leaders of network subgroups closer together does not facilitate consensus. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that increasing noise in the information network makes it less likely for groups to reach consensus and increases the time required to do so. The geodesic distance between leaders of opposing subgroups does not have a significant effect on the probability or speed of consensus formation. Leadership labeling affects individual behavior, making leaders more likely to compromise when their neighbors do, but this does not translate into group-level differences in consensus based on leader distance. The study does not analyze or report any efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The experimental design does not include punishment, sanctions, or any efficiency/payoff-based outcome. The findings are specific to consensus formation in a networked coordination game with leader labeling and noise, and do not transfer to the prediction of efficiency under punishment in PGG or similar games.

- source: Cesarini, D., Dawes, C. T., Fowler, J. H., Johannesson, M., Lichtenstein, P., & Wallace, B. (2008). Heritability of cooperative behavior in the trust game. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that individual differences in trust and trustworthiness in the trust game are partially explained by genetic variation, with heritability estimates ranging from 10-20% for trust and 17-18% for trustworthiness. The majority of behavioral variance is due to unshared environmental factors. There is little evidence that common environment (e.g., family upbringing) explains much of the variance. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it analyze efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. It does not include punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes, and the game studied (trust game) is only adjacent to public goods games. The findings are only relevant for understanding the sources of individual variation in cooperative behavior, not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment or game design dimensions.

- source: Capraro, V., & Marcelletti, A. (2014). Do good actions inspire good actions in others?. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that good actions (altruism or benevolence) do not inspire further good actions in anonymous, one-shot economic games. Specifically, being the recipient of a good action (either a split in a Dictator Game or a benevolent act in a Benevolence Game) does not significantly increase the probability of cooperating in a subsequent Prisoner's Dilemma or splitting in a subsequent Dictator Game. The results are robust to exclusion of participants who did not believe the other player was real or failed to provide a valid reason for their choice. No efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. Its findings on the lack of behavioral contagion of good actions in one-shot, anonymous settings may be contextually relevant for understanding baseline cooperation or altruism, but not for the prediction of efficiency under punishment treatments.

- source: Miranda-Rodríguez, R. A., Leenen, I., Han, H., Palafox-Palafox, G., & García-Rodríguez, G. (2023). Moral reasoning and moral competence as predictors of cooperative behavior in a social dilemma. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in an iterated stranger PDG, the probability of cooperation is strongly influenced by the outcome of previous rounds: negative experiences (sucker, punishment, temptation) reduce cooperation, while positive experiences (reward) increase it. However, individuals with higher levels of moral reasoning (DIT-2 N2-index) and moral competence (MCT C-score) are less likely to reduce cooperation after negative experiences, particularly after sucker outcomes. There is no main effect of moral reasoning or competence on overall cooperation rates, but both moderate the effect of negative experiences. The study does not examine efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism, nor does it analyze efficiency or group payoff. Its findings are limited to individual-level predictors of cooperation in a repeated PDG without punishment or reward. The study is only indirectly relevant for understanding baseline cooperation dynamics in the absence of punishment, and does not inform how punishment would affect efficiency or payoff outcomes under different game design dimensions.

- source: Candia, C., Oyarzún, M., Landaeta, V., Yaikin, T., Monge, C., Hidalgo, C., & Rodriguez-Sickert, C. (2022). Reciprocity heightens academic performance in elementary school students. *Heliyon*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that students who engage in more reciprocated cooperative relationships (as measured by a non-anonymous, continuous-token Prisoner's Dilemma game) tend to have higher subsequent academic performance (GPA). This association is robust to controls for prior GPA, attendance, social status, and other confounders, and is confirmed using a difference-in-differences approach. The effect is stronger for students in the top 20% of reciprocated cooperation. The experiment does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes from the game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The experimental design lacks any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and the outcomes are not efficiency or payoff-based but rather focus on mapping social relationships and predicting academic performance. The game is a non-anonymous, dyadic, continuous-token social dilemma, which is adjacent to PGGs but not a direct variant. Thus, this paper should not be used to inform predictions about punishment effects on efficiency in PGGs or similar games.

- source: van Apeldoorn, J., & Schram, A. (2016). Indirect Reciprocity; A Field Experiment. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper provides field evidence that downstream indirect reciprocity operates in an online community: members with a history of helping others are more likely to have their own requests for help accepted. This effect is robust across most subgroups. There is no evidence for upstream indirect reciprocity. The study does not involve a public goods game or any form of punishment or sanctioning, nor does it report on efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not involve punishment, sanctions, or payoff-based outcomes, and the experimental design is not a PGG or close variant. Its findings on indirect reciprocity may be of theoretical interest for understanding cooperation, but provide no direct or indirect evidence for the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Rand, D. G., & Epstein, Z. G. (2014). Risking Your Life without a Second Thought: Intuitive Decision-Making and Extreme Altruism. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=weak | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The main finding is that individuals who engage in extreme altruism overwhelmingly describe their decision-making as intuitive, fast, and automatic, rather than deliberative or reasoned. This is supported both by human ratings of interview statements and by linguistic analysis of inhibitory language. The results are robust even in cases where the altruist had time to deliberate. The study does not involve any economic games, public goods games, punishment, or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze any of the game design dimensions of interest (e.g., player count, punishment, MPCR, etc.), nor does it report on efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings about intuitive decision-making in real-world extreme altruism may be of theoretical interest for understanding the psychology of cooperation, but do not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment and efficiency in public goods games.

- source: Radkani, S., Tenenbaum, J. B., & Saxe, R. (2025). What people learn from punishment: A cognitive model. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that observers' inferences about the wrongness of an act and the motives of an authority after observing punishment are interdependent and strongly shaped by prior beliefs about norms and authority. Punishment can communicate norm violation and enhance or damage the authority's reputation, depending on context. The authors' Bayesian inverse planning model quantitatively predicts these belief updates and outperforms alternative models. The study also shows that repeated observation of punishment can either reduce or sustain polarization in beliefs, depending on initial priors. However, all outcomes are belief-based; the study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or actual behavioral cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are about belief updating and interpretation of punishment, not about actual contributions, cooperation, or payoffs. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about treatment efficiency from game design dimensions or control efficiency. It may be useful for understanding how punishment is perceived or how norm communication and legitimacy are inferred, but not for payoff-based outcome prediction.

- source: Gächter, S., Molleman, L., & Nosenzo, D. (2025). Why people follow rules. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that 55-70% of participants follow an arbitrary costly rule even when acting anonymously and alone, and when rule-breaking has no consequences for others. Social expectations and intrinsic respect for rules explain most rule-following. Adding externalities (harm to a charity) or punishment (with varying probabilities) increases rule-following rates, but a substantial baseline of rule-following exists without these. Observing peer rule-breaking reduces rule-following, but even with all peers breaking the rule, a significant fraction still comply. The main outcome is behavioral (rule-following), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar group cooperation settings. While it provides evidence that punishment increases rule-following in a single-player, non-strategic, asocial rule-following task, the outcome is not efficiency or group payoff, and the context is not a PGG or its variant. The findings may be relevant for understanding the motivational basis of compliance and the effect of sanctions on rule-following, but cannot be directly transferred to efficiency predictions in PGG-like environments.

- source: Clavien, C., Tanner, C. J., Clément, F., & Chapuisat, M. (2012). Choosy Moral Punishers. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that the propensity to punish a norm violator (by voting against her) varies by socio-professional category: future teachers are significantly more likely to punish than future police officers or high school students, despite all groups recognizing the immoral behavior. The punishment is costless and anonymous, and the context is a simulated music competition, not an economic or public goods game. The findings challenge the idea of a universal, context-independent motivation to punish norm violators. No efficiency, payoff, or group welfare outcomes are measured or discussed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or similar economic games. Its findings are about context-specific willingness to punish in a non-economic, real-world scenario, and do not inform the relationship between punishment and efficiency or payoff. It may be relevant for understanding moderators of punishment motivation, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on efficiency in PGG-like environments.

- source: Peperkoorn, L. S., Becker, D. V., Balliet, D., Columbus, S., Molho, C., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2020). The prevalence of dyads in social life. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: Across five studies (four surveys and one experience-sampling), dyads are consistently the most common group size reported for a variety of social activities in both the US and the Netherlands. The paper finds some small sex differences (women are sometimes more dyadic), and minor associations with age and relationship status, but the main result is the predominance of dyads. The discussion connects this to theories of reciprocity, coordination, and social exclusion, but does not empirically address cooperation, punishment, or efficiency in economic games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide empirical evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not report on any economic game, nor does it analyze efficiency, payoff, or punishment. Its only indirect relevance is in highlighting that most real-world social interactions occur in dyads, which may inform the ecological validity of two-player game studies versus larger group games. However, it does not provide any data or analysis that would inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions or control efficiency.

- source: Jordan, J. J., & Kouchaki, M. (2021). Virtuous victims. *Science Advances*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The main finding is that people perceive victims of immorality as more moral and trustworthy than nonvictims, even when their behavior is identical. This effect is specific to moral traits and does not extend to nonmoral traits or to victims of accidents. The effect is explained by the Justice Restoration Hypothesis: seeing victims as virtuous motivates people to help them and punish perpetrators. The effect disappears when there are disincentives for justice-restorative action. The paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or actual behavioral outcomes in economic games, but does show that perceived victim virtue increases willingness to punish and help in hypothetical contexts.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public-goods-game-like environments with or without punishment. It does not manipulate or measure group payoff, efficiency, or actual economic outcomes. However, it provides adjacent evidence that willingness to punish is motivated by perceptions of victim virtue, and that social incentives can modulate this willingness. For the prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, but it may be contextually relevant for understanding psychological motivations behind punishment and justice-restorative actions.

- source: Neilands, P., Hassall, R., Derks, F., Bastos, A. P. M., & Taylor, A. H. (2020). Watching eyes do not stop dogs stealing food: evidence against a general risk-aversion hypothesis for the watching-eye effect. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: Dogs are sensitive to owner commands and are slower to take forbidden food when told to 'leave' compared to 'go.' However, the presence of images of eyes does not further increase their latency to steal food compared to control images. This suggests that the watching-eye effect, robustly observed in humans, does not generalize to dogs, even though dogs are highly sensitive to human eye cues in other contexts. The findings argue against a general risk-aversion explanation for the watching-eye effect and support the idea that it is linked to human-specific reputation management. No efficiency, payoff, or group-level outcomes are measured or discussed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar economic games. The study is about animal behavior (dogs) in a food-stealing paradigm, not about human cooperation, punishment, or efficiency in economic games. While the discussion references punishment and reputation management in humans, these are not empirically tested in this study. Therefore, this paper should not be used to inform predictions about efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment.

- source: Exadaktylos, F., Espín, A. M., & Brañas-Garza, P. (2013). Experimental subjects are not different. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that both student status and self-selection (volunteering) can have small effects on social preference behaviors, but these effects are generally minor and not economically significant. Self-selected students (the typical lab subject pool) behave very similarly to the broader population in Dictator, Ultimatum, and Trust Games. The only significant differences are small and mainly concern strategic behavior and trust/reciprocity among non-students. The results support the external validity of using self-selected students for social preference experiments, but do not address public goods games, punishment, or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include any public goods game, punishment, or sanctioning treatment, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Its findings are only relevant to the representativeness of subject pools in social preference experiments using Dictator, Ultimatum, and Trust Games. It should not be used to inform predictions about punishment effects on efficiency in PGG-like settings.

- source: Panasiti, M. S., Pavone, E. F., Merla, A., & Aglioti, S. M. (2011). Situational and Dispositional Determinants of Intentional Deceiving. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that participants are more likely to lie for self-gain when facing an unfavorable outcome, but this tendency is reduced when their reputation is at risk (i.e., when the opponent will know about their lies). The presence of the opponent in the same room does not significantly affect lying rates. Personality traits, particularly reward dependence and social desirability, predict the degree to which reputation risk reduces lying. Manipulativeness and moral disengagement are associated with less sensitivity to reputation risk. The study does not involve punishment, sanctions, or public goods game structures, and does not report on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. Its findings are limited to the behavioral determinants of lying under reputation risk and personality variation in a dyadic deception game. It should not be used to inform predictions about punishment effects on efficiency in PGG-like settings.

- source: Manesi, Z., Van Lange, P. A. M., & Pollet, T. V. (2015). Butterfly Eyespots: Their Potential Influence on Aesthetic Preferences and Conservation Attitudes. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that minimal eye-like patterns (eyespots) on butterflies increase their perceived attractiveness and elicit more positive attitudes toward their conservation. In some cases, eyespots also increase the likelihood of actual conservation behavior (e.g., donations), though this effect is mixed. The effect of eyespots on conservation attitudes is mediated by increased aesthetic preference. The study does not address economic games, punishment, or group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze any of the game design dimensions relevant to public goods games, nor does it report payoff-based outcomes. Its findings are not transferable to the prediction task at hand.
